NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {BLA 761344}
IMDELLTRA - Tarlatamab
Applicant Proposed IMDELLTRA is indicated for the treatment of adult patients with
Indication(s)/Population(s) advanced small cell lung cancer (SCLC) with disease progression
on or after platinum-based chemotherapy
Recommendation on Accelerated Approval
Regulatory Action
Recommended IMDELLTRA is indicated for the treatment of adult patients with
Indication(s)/Population(s) extensive stage small cell lung cancer (ES-SCLC) with disease
(if applicable) progression on or after platinum-based chemotherapy

12.3 Pharmacokinetics ............................................................................................................ 291
Effects of Tarlatamab on CYP450 Substrates.......................................................................... 292
Tarlatamab causes transient release of cytokines that may suppress CYP450 enzymes and
may result in an increased exposure of concomitant CYP substrates during and up to 14 days
after occurrence of cytokine release syndrome [see Clinical Pharmacology (12.2)]. ............ 292

IMDELLTRA - Tarlatamab

1 Executive Summary
1.1.

Product Introduction

Tarlatamab-dlle (herein referred to as tarlatamab) is a bispecific T-cell engager that binds to
delta-like ligand 3 (DLL3) expressed on the surface of cells, including tumor cells, and CD3
expressed on the surface of T-cells. Tarlatamab causes T-cell activation, release of inflammatory
cytokines and lysis of DLL3-expressing cells. Tarlatamab is a new molecular entity and is not
currently approved in the United States or elsewhere.
In this biologics licensing application (BLA), Amgen’s proposed indication for tarlatamab is “for
the treatment of adult patients with advanced small cell lung cancer (SCLC) with disease
progression on or after platinum-based chemotherapy.”
The recommended dosing regimen for the proposed indication is an initial dose of tarlatamab
of 1 mg on Day 1 followed by 10 mg on Days 8 and 15 and every 2 weeks thereafter.

1.2.

Conclusions on the Substantial Evidence of Effectiveness

The FDA review teams recommend accelerated approval of tarlatamab for the following
indication: Tarlatamab is indicated for the treatment of adult patients with extensive stage
small cell lung cancer (ES-SCLC) with disease progression on or after platinum-based
chemotherapy.
The recommendation for accelerated approval, according to 21 CFR 314.510 Subpart H, is based
on the results from Parts 1 and 2 of Study 20200491 (DeLLphi-301), an ongoing, multicenter,
open-label, multi-cohort trial evaluating the efficacy and safety of tarlatamab as a single agent
in adult patients with relapsed/refractory SCLC with disease progression after receiving
previous treatment with platinum-based chemotherapy and at least one other line of prior
therapy. The assessment of safety was also supported by results from Part 3 of Study 20200491
and from Study 20160323, an ongoing first-in-human (FIH), open-label, ascending, multiple
dose study of tarlatamab in adult patients with advanced SCLC who have progressed or
recurred after at least one platinum-based regimen.
The primary efficacy population consists of 99 patients enrolled in Parts 1 and 2 of Study
20200491 who received tarlatamab at the dosage proposed for marketing (initial dose of 1 mg
of on Cycle 1 Day 1 followed by 10 mg on Days 8, 15, and every 2 weeks thereafter). Of the 99
patients, 73 patients (74%) had received prior anti-PD-(L)1 therapy; of these 73 patients, 58
(59%) received anti-PD-(L)1 therapy in combination with platinum-based chemotherapy in the
20
Version date: June 2022 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)
Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the
Applicant and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.
Reference ID: 5382024

NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {BLA 761344}
IMDELLTRA - Tarlatamab
first line setting. The confirmed blinded independent central review (BICR)-assessed overall
response rate (ORR), as per Response Evaluation Criteria in Solid Tumors version 1.1 (RECIST
v1.1), was 40% (95% CI: 31%, 51%). The median duration of response (DoR) was 9.7 months
(range 2.7 to 20.7+ months); 68% of responders had an observed DoR ≥6 months and 40% of
responders had an observed DoR ≥12 months.
FDA considers response rate, supported by DoR, an appropriate early clinical endpoint for SCLC
because it is a direct measure of the intervention; in the absence of therapy, tumors do not
typically regress on their own. The observed ORR and DoR were clinically meaningful, and
responses were observed across subgroups of patients with platinum-sensitive and platinumrefractory disease and across subgroups with various tumor DLL3 expression cut-offs.
Cytotoxic chemotherapies administered after disease has progressed on platinum-based
chemotherapy are expected to have differential tumor response based on a patient’s
“sensitivity” to platinum-based chemotherapy, with very low response rates reported for
patients with platinum resistant SCLC. Given this, efficacy has typically been assessed separately
in subgroups of patients with platinum-sensitive or platinum-resistant SCLC; however, given the
known mechanism of action of tarlatamab as a targeted agent that causes activation of the
immune system, which differs from cytotoxic chemotherapy, platinum sensitivity is not
expected to be a predictive factor for response to tarlatamab. With this caveat, it was still
considered appropriate to evaluate ORR in these subgroups.
Among the 99 patients who received tarlatamab at the proposed dosage in Study 20200491, 69
patients had available data regarding platinum sensitivity status. In this subgroup of patients,
ORR was 31% (95% CI: 18%, 47%) in 42 patients with platinum-sensitive SCLC (defined as ≥90
days after end of first line platinum therapy to date of progression) and 52% (95%: CI 32%, 71%)
in 27 patients with platinum-resistant SCLC (defined as < 90 days after end of first line platinum
therapy to date of progression). Although the ORR point estimates differ between these
subgroups, the 95% confidence intervals overlap, and FDA considers that a true difference in
efficacy based on platinum sensitivity status is unlikely with tarlatamab.
For this application, the proposed indication, SCLC with disease progression on or after
platinum-based chemotherapy, differs from the population enrolled in Study 20200491,
patients with SCLC with disease progression after receiving previous treatment with platinumbased chemotherapy and at least one other line of prior therapy. Overall, the ORR and DoR data
from the primary efficacy population in Study 20200491, which enrolled a more heavily treated
patient population (3rd line or greater), is greater than the ORR and DoR observed with
topotecan, the only available therapy with traditional approval from the FDA for patients with
SCLC previously treated with first-line chemotherapy (ORR of 24% in platinum-sensitive disease
and 2-7% in platinum-resistant disease). Review of prior therapies in the primary efficacy
population from Study 20200491 showed that 52% of patients received a topoisomerase I

the subgroups of patients who received prior topoisomerase I inhibitor and who did not receive
prior topoisomerase I inhibitor (including and not including patients who received standard of

observed in the overall study population. Furthermore, it is not expected that treatment with
additional therapies following platinum-based chemotherapy would increase the likelihood of
response to tarlatamab relative to treatment with tarlatamab used as second-line therapy
following platinum-based chemotherapy. In light of the observed results and particularly
considering the ORR observed with tarlatamab in patients with platinum-resistant SCLC, which
far exceeds ORR reported with standard of care therapies, the available data for tarlatamab
supports the proposed indication for treatment of SCLC with disease progression on or after
platinum-based chemotherapy.
Overall, the ORR and durable responses observed with tarlatamab in Study 20200491 are
consistent with a meaningful advantage over available therapies for the proposed indication
and provide substantial evidence of effectiveness and support a recommendation of
accelerated approval for tarlatamab for the following indication: treatment of adult patients
with extensive-stage small cell lung cancer (ES-SCLC) with disease progression on or after
platinum-based chemotherapy.

1.3.

Benefit-Risk Assessment (BRA)

Benefit-Risk Summary and Assessment

Lung cancer is the leading cause of cancer-related mortality in the US and worldwide (Siegel, 2023). Approximately 15% of lung cancers
diagnosed are small cell lung cancer (American Cancer Society, 2024). The majority of patients with SCLC have extensive-stage SCLC (ES-SCLC) at
presentation. Current first-line treatment for ES-SCLC generally consists of etoposide in combination with platinum chemotherapy and a
programmed death ligand 1 (PD-L1) immune checkpoint inhibitor (atezolizumab or durvalumab). Although the response rate to initial
treatment is high with ORR of 60-70%, nearly all patients relapse after initial therapy and the median overall survival from front-line ES-SCLC
trials is approximately 12 to 13 months (Liu 2021, Paz-Ares 2022). The 5-year survival for ES-SCLC is less than 2% (Basumallik 2023). Depending
on the interval between completion of chemotherapy and detection of relapse, treatment options include retreatment with a platinum-based
regimen or use of single agent chemotherapy.
Topotecan is the only therapy with traditional approval in the US for patients with SCLC whose disease has progressed after first-line
chemotherapy; the approved indication is for platinum-sensitive SCLC, with topotecan demonstrating an overall response rate (ORR) of 24%
(95% CI: 16%, 32%) with a median duration of response (DoR) of 3.3 months. There is no therapy with traditional approval for patients with
platinum-resistant SCLC. Lurbinectedin has received accelerated approval for the treatment of adult patients with metastatic SCLC whose
disease has progressed on or after platinum-based chemotherapy based on an ORR as assessed by independent review committee of 30% (95%
CI: 22%, 40%) with a median DoR of 5.1 months (95% CI: 4.9, 6.4). The ORR was 43% (95% CI 31%, 57%) in the platinum-sensitive subgroup and
13% (95% CI 5%, 27%) in the platinum-resistant subgroup. In addition to topotecan and lurbinectedin, other therapeutic options that are not
FDA approved include taxanes, irinotecan, doxorubicin, gemcitabine, and temozolomide.
Tarlatamab is a bispecific delta-like ligand 3 (DLL3)-directed CD3 T-cell engager. Tarlatamab causes T-cell activation, production of inflammatory
cytokines and release of cytotoxic proteins which results in redirected lysis of DLL3-expressing cells. Currently, tarlatamab is not approved in the
US for any indication.
The evaluation of the efficacy of tarlatamab is based on results from Parts 1 and 2 of Study 20200491 (DeLLphi-301 trial), an ongoing,
multicenter, open-label, multi-cohort trial evaluating the efficacy and safety of tarlatamab as a single agent in adult patients with

relapsed/refractory SCLC with disease progression after receiving previous treatment with platinum-based chemotherapy and at least one
other line of prior therapy. The assessment of safety was also supported by results from Part 3 of Study 20200491 and from Study 20160323, an
ongoing first-in-human (FIH), open-label, ascending, multiple dose study of tarlatamab in adult patients with advanced SCLC who have
progressed or recurred after at least one platinum-based regimen.
The primary efficacy population consists of 99 patients enrolled in Parts 1 and 2 of Study 20200491 who received tarlatamab at the dosage
proposed for marketing (initial dose of 1 mg of on Cycle 1 Day 1 followed by 10 mg on Days 8, 15, and every 2 weeks thereafter). Of the 99
patients, 73 patients (74%) had received prior anti-PD-(L)1 therapy. Of these 73 patients, 58 (59%) received anti-PD-(L)1 therapy in combination
with platinum-based chemotherapy in the first line setting. The confirmed blinded independent central review (BICR)-assessed ORR, as per
Response Evaluation Criteria in Solid Tumors version 1.1 (RECIST v1.1), was 40% (95% CI: 31%, 51%). The median duration of response (DoR)
was 9.7 months (range 2.7 to 20.7+ months); 68% of responders had an observed DoR ≥6 months and 40% of responders had an observed DoR

subgroups, the 95% confidence intervals overlap, and FDA considers that a true difference in efficacy based on platinum sensitivity status is

IMDELLTRA - Tarlatamab

2 Therapeutic Context
2.1.

Analysis of Condition

The Applicant’s Position:
Globally, lung cancer is the second most commonly diagnosed cancer and leading cause of
cancer death, representing approximately 1 in 10 (11.4%) cancers diagnosed and 1 in 5 (18.0%)
cancer deaths (Sung et al, 2021). Small cell lung cancer (SCLC) accounts for approximately 15%
of overall lung cancer cases with approximately 330 000 new cases worldwide each year based
on an estimated 2.2 million cases of lung cancer in 2020 (National Cancer Institute [NCI], 2023;
National Organization for Rare Disorders, 2021; Rudin et al, 2021; Sung et al, 2021). There are
approximately 270 000 new deaths worldwide due to SCLC based on approximately 1.8 million
lung cancer deaths in 2020 (Sung et al, 2021).
Small cell lung cancer is a high-grade neuroendocrine tumor marked by an exceptionally high
proliferative rate, strong predilection for early metastasis, and poor prognosis (Rudin et al,
2021). While 30% of patients present with disease that can be encompassed by one
radiotherapy field (limited stage), the majority of cases have disease diagnosed as extensive
stage. Although 20% to 30% of patients with limited-stage SCLC can be cured with
radio-chemotherapy, treatment is rarely curative in extensive stage, and SCLC is associated with
poor long-term survival (5-year survival is < 10%) (American Cancer Society, 2022).
(b) (4)

34
Version date: June 2022 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)
Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the
Applicant and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.

Reference ID: 5382024

NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {BLA 761344}
IMDELLTRA - Tarlatamab
Overall, the results from the
published literature show that
patients with SCLC have poor treatment outcomes with existing therapies in second line or
later, and their prognosis in the overall population and in the subset treated with first-line
anti-PD-L1 therapy is poor. Therefore, there remains a significant unmet medical need in
patients with advanced SCLC for novel therapies that can support longer survival and reduce
disease-associated symptoms with an acceptable safety profile. Tarlatamab is being developed
with the goal of addressing this unmet need.
(b) (4)

The FDA’s Assessment:
FDA agrees with the Applicant’s position.

2.2. Analysis of Current Treatment Options

The Applicant’s Position:

Current first-line treatment of SCLC generally consists of etoposide in combination with either
cisplatin or carboplatin with the addition of radiation for limited-stage SCLC and the addition of
a PD-L1 immune checkpoint inhibitor (atezolizumab or durvalumab) for extensive-stage SCLC
(National Comprehensive Cancer Network [NCCN] SCLC Guidelines, 2023; Guidelines for
Diagnosis and Treatment of Lung Cancer in Japan, 2022; Rudin et al, 2021; Ganti et al, 2021).
Although this first-line regimen in extensive-stage SCLC has a relatively high response rate of
60% to 70%, resistance and relapse almost always occur, usually within the first year after
treatment for nearly all patients with extensive-stage SCLC (Rudin et al, 2021; Yoon et al, 2019;
Chan and Coward, 2013), and median OS is approximately 12 to 13 months (Tariq et al, 2021;
Paz-Ares et al, 2019; Horn et al, 2018).
Treatment options for patients with relapsed SCLC are limited, and with currently available
therapy, prognosis remains poor. In addition to consideration of platinum rechallenge or clinical
trial participation, other recommended regimens in the relapsed or refractory setting include
topotecan (Eckardt et al, 2007; O’Brien et al, 2006; von Pawel et al, 1999), lurbinectedin (Trigo
et al, 2020), other chemotherapy agents (eg, cyclophosphamide/doxorubicin/vincristine,
docetaxel, etoposide, gemcitabine, temozolomide), or immunotherapy agents (eg, nivolumab,
pembrolizumab).
National Comprehensive Cancer Network guidelines include several therapies as other
recommended regimens for patients who progress subsequent to initial systemic therapy;
however, only topotecan is Food and Drug Administration (FDA) approved for use in patients
with SCLC platinum-sensitive disease who progress at least 60 days after initiation of first-line
chemotherapy (NCCN, 2023) (Table 1). Of note, topotecan was approved based on a study
comparing topotecan to cyclophosphamide, doxorubicin, and vincristine combination
35
Version date: June 2022 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)
Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the
Applicant and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.

Reference ID: 5382024

NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {BLA 761344}
IMDELLTRA - Tarlatamab
chemotherapy, which did not show statistically significant improvements in response rate,
duration of response (DOR), time to progression, or OS (topotecan US Prescribing Information,
2019; von Pawel et al, 1999). In the study, topotecan demonstrated an objective response rate
(ORR) (95% CI) of 24.3% (16.2%, 32.4%) and median (95% CI) DOR of 3.3 (3.0, 4.1) months
(Table 1); cyclophosphamide, doxorubicin, and vincristine combination chemotherapy
demonstrated an ORR (95% CI) of 18.3% (10.8%, 25.7%) and median (95% CI) DOR of 3.5 (3.0,
5.3) months.
National Comprehensive Cancer Network guidelines (2023) also include lurbinectedin as a
recommended regimen in patients who progress subsequent to initial systemic therapy with
platinum-based chemotherapy (NCCN, 2023). Lurbinectedin received accelerated approval in
August 2020 based on an ORR (95% CI) as assessed by investigator of 35% (26%, 45%) and
median (95% CI) DOR of 5.3 (4.1, 6.4) months (Zepzelca US Prescribing Information, 2023). It is
important to note that all subjects enrolled in this study had progressed on or after
platinum-based chemotherapy, with only 8% of subjects also having prior immunotherapy.
Recent novel therapeutic strategies such as immunotherapy with programmed cell death 1
(PD-1) checkpoint inhibitors have not improved outcomes compared with topotecan in the
second-line or later setting (Das et al, 2021; Giffin et al, 2021; Trigo et al, 2020), and the need
for durable responses remains high. Additionally, the benefits of immunotherapy in first-line
treatment of extensive-stage SCLC are relatively modest (approximately 2 to 3 month increase
in median OS) (Paz-Ares et al, 2019; Horn et al, 2018) compared with that observed in
non-small cell lung cancer in first-line treatment. Small cell lung cancer is considered an
immune-cold lung cancer subtype due to the limited infiltration of immune cells, reduced PD-L1
expression, and lack of antigen presentation (Caliman et al, 2023; Sutherland et al, 2022).
Although SCLC has a high tumor mutational burden related to the carcinogenic activity of
tobacco exposure, this does not appear to translate to a similar favorable response to
PD-L1 and PD-1 inhibitors as in non-small cell lung cancer (Sutherland et al, 2022) and other
alternative immunotherapeutic approaches may be of interest.
A meta-analysis of 27 studies, enrolling a total of 2792 patients, suggests heterogeneity in the
prevalence of PD-L1 expression across studies. No statistical association has been confirmed,
although positive PD-L1 expression seems to be a favorable prognostic factor for SCLC
(Acheampong et al, 2020). The sensitivity of immunotherapy may be associated with the
disease itself, according to the molecular classification biology of SCLC (Caliman et al, 2023).
Further studies are necessary to determine which patient groups would benefit the most from
immunotherapy treatment.
Currently there are no approved therapies for patients failing 2 or more prior lines of therapy
(NCCN SCLC Guidelines, 2023; Giffin et al, 2021). Patients receiving best supportive care in third
line had an extremely poor survival outcome with a median OS of 0.9 months, whereas patients
receiving systemic therapy in third line had a median OS of 4.4 months (Coutinho et al, 2019).

The FDA’s Assessment:
FDA generally agrees with the Applicant’s presentation of current treatment options. Cytotoxic
chemotherapies are expected to have differential tumor response based on a patient’s
“sensitivity” to platinum-based chemotherapy and given this, efficacy is usually assessed
separately in subgroups of platinum-sensitive or platinum-resistant with the former group
being generally defined as patients who have had disease progression ≥ 90 days after
completion of first-line platinum-based chemotherapy and the latter group being defined as
patients who have progression < 90 days after completion of first-line platinum-based
chemotherapy. Based on data from single arm studies with topotecan using the more standard
cut-off of ≥90 days for platinum-sensitive vs. platinum-resistant, reported response rates
ranged from 11-31% for patients with platinum-sensitive disease and 2-7% in patients with
platinum-resistant disease. For lurbinectedin, accelerated approval was granted based on an
ORR of 35%; ORR was 43% (95% CI: 31%, 57%) in the platinum-sensitive subgroup and 13%
(95% CI: 5%, 27%) in the platinum-resistant subgroup.

Inspections of Drs. Cho and Font’s sites also revealed unreported AEs that are unlikely to
significantly affect overall reliability of safety or efficacy data for these sites. The large number
of underreported AEs by the Korean sites, not captured during the Applicant’s monitoring
activities, is a major concern and put into question the integrity of the safety data submitted to
support the approval of tarlatamab for the proposed indication.
The Applicant has acknowledged the deficiencies and decided to further expand their review to
conduct 100% source data verification (SDV)/source data review (SDR) for all sites involved in
20200491. Based on SDR of imaging records, 25 previously unsubmitted scans were identified
in 17 patients. Of the 25 scans, 23 were unscheduled scans and were previously not submitted
as these scans were completed as part of standard of care or for patient AE assessment. Of the
remaining 2 scans, one was a scheduled brain MRI that was not submitted for central review
due to a technical imaging upload error and the other was a baseline abdominal MRI that was
not submitted as the site erroneously indicated that only baseline CT was available.
These unsubmitted scans were submitted to BICR for review and from this the Applicant
updated the ORR by BICR from 40% to 41% for the tarlatamab 10 mg group and from 32% to
33% in the tarlatamab 100 mg group. The median DoR did not change in the 10 mg group and
changed from 8.1 to 7.6 months in the 100 mg group. However, upon further review by FDA,
none of the newly identified scans that were submitted factored into the update of response
for these patients and the update of ORR was from re-adjudication of previous target lesion
assessments. Given this, FDA did not agree to update the ORR the DoR summary statistics
would be based on the previous ORR by BICR of 40%.

5 Nonclinical Pharmacology/Toxicology
5.1. Executive Summary

Tarlatamab (AMG 757) is a bispecific T cell engager that binds to delta-like ligand 3 (DLL3) and
CD3. DLL3 is a Notch ligand that is expressed during fetal development but has limited
expression on the cell surface in healthy adult tissues. DLL3 has also been reported to be
expressed on the cell surface of some cancers, including small cell lung cancer (SCLC). CD3 is
expressed on T cells, and upon simultaneously binding to the surface of DLL3-expressing target
cells and CD3 on T cells, tarlatamab promotes T cell activation and T cell-dependent lysis of
DLL3-expressing cells, including SCLC cells. The Applicant proposes to use tarlatamab at 1 mg on
Day 1, then 10 mg on Days 8, 15, and every 2 weeks thereafter. The proposed indication is for
the treatment of adult patients with SCLC with disease progression on or after platinum-based
chemotherapy.
As assessed by surface plasmon resonance (SPR), tarlatamab bound human and monkey CD3
with KDs of 14.9 nM and 12 nM, respectively. Tarlatamab bound CHO cells expressing human or
monkey DLL3 with KDs of 0.64 nM and 0.5 nM, respectively. Tarlatamab also bound human and
monkey T cells at a concentration of 50 ng/mL but did not bind T cells from mice, rats, rabbits,
dogs, or minipigs. The cynomolgus monkey was selected for toxicology testing given that
tarlatamab similarly bound human and monkey CD3 and DLL3 but did not bind CD3 from other
species. However, the monkey may not be pharmacologically relevant considering DLL3 is
expressed intracellularly in normal cells and tissues. It is unknown if other conditions or disease
states can lead to expression of DLL3 on the surface of some cells.
The ability of tarlatamab to activate T cells and mediate T cell cytotoxicity was evaluated in in
vitro assays. In co-cultures with human peripheral blood mononuclear cells (PBMCs) and DLL3expressing human small cell lung cancer cell lines, tarlatamab resulted in upregulation of T cell
activation markers and cytokine release, as well as cytotoxicity of DLL3-postive target cells.
Tarlatamab mediated T cell lysis of several human SCLC cell lines with EC50s of 32 pM to 161
pM. Additionally, the ability of tarlatamab to mediate cytotoxicity of DLL3-postive target cells
positively correlated with levels of DLL3 expressed on the surface of target cells. Tarlatamab
also demonstrated ability to activate CD4 and CD8 T cells in co-cultures with DLL3-expressing
human cancer cell lines as assessed by increased expression of T cell activation markers, such as
CD25 and CD69, and cytotoxicity markers, perforin and granzyme B, as well as release of
cytokines, including the proinflammatory cytokines IL-2, IL-6, TNF-α, and IFNγ. Tarlatamab did
not mediate cytokine release from human PBMCs when co-cultured with target cells lacking
expression of DLL3.

Version date: June 2022 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)

47

Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the
Applicant and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.

Reference ID: 5382024

NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {BLA 761344}
{Tarlatamab}
In the presence of dexamethasone, a glucocorticoid used clinically to antagonize severe
inflammatory reactions, the ability of tarlatamab to mediate T cell activation and cytotoxicity of
DLL3-expressing human SCLC cell lines was reduced approximately 3-fold. Tarlatamab-mediated
release of IFNγ, TNF-α, IL-10, and IL-6 were also reduced in co-cultures of human PBMCs and
SCLC cell lines compared to cultures that were not treated with dexamethasone.
Consistent with its mutated Fc region, tarlatamab demonstrated low affinity binding to FcγRI
and no binding to FcγRII, FcγRIII, or C1q, thus tarlatamab is not expected to mediate Fc-effector
functions; the mutations in the Fc region did not affect the ability of tarlatamab to bind the
neonatal Fc receptor (FcRn). In an in vitro assay, tarlatamab resulted in low level upregulation
of activation markers CD25 and CD69 on human T cells when PBMCs were cultured alone or in
the presence of HCT-116 cells lacking expression of DLL3, suggesting the potential for
tarlatamab to mediate T cell activation independently of DLL3 engagement via binding of the
ScFc region of tarlatamab to FcγRI on target cells and simultaneous binding to CD3 on T cells;
however, upregulation of CD25 and CD69 were abolished in the presence of physiological levels
of human IgG. This finding suggests that competitive binding from human IgG can block
tarlatamab binding to FcγRs, thereby inhibiting target-independent T cell activation. Notably,
antigen-independent upregulation of T cell activation markers did not correlate with cytokine
release from unstimulated PBMCs in an in vitro assay described above, further indicating that
potential interactions with FcγRI are weak and are unlikely to lead to T cell activation or Fcmediated effector functions.
Tarlatamab inhibited tumor growth in multiple mouse tumor models. Because tarlatamab does
not bind mouse CD3, the in vivo anti-tumor activity of tarlatamab was evaluated using
immunodeficient mice administered human PBMCs or T cells by IV infusion prior to treatment
with tarlatamab. In orthotopic models of SCLC, tarlatamab inhibited tumor growth when
administered at doses ≥0.1 mg/kg once weekly via intraperitoneal injection. Anti-tumor activity
in SCLC models was associated with expanded CD4 and CD8 T cell populations in tumors from
mice administered tarlatamab compared to tumors from mice treated with an isotype control
bispecific antibody. Overall, based on primary pharmacology data the established
pharmacologic class (EPC) of tartalamab is bispecific delta-like ligand (DLL3)-directed CD3 T-cell
engager.
The Applicant evaluated the safety of tarlatamab administered intravenously, the intended
route of administration, using cynomolgus monkeys in GLP-compliant 1- and 3-month repeatdose toxicology studies, as well as a non-GLP 10-day study. No mortalities occurred in these
studies at doses up to 4500 µg/kg, the maximal feasible dose that could be administered to
monkeys. Findings in these studies included decreases in lymphocytes at doses ≥1000 µg/kg in
all repeat-dose toxicology studies in monkeys; this finding improved after a drug-free period.
Changes in lymphocytes also correlated with decreases in total CD3, CD4, and CD8 T cell
Version date: June 2022 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)

48

Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the
Applicant and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.

Reference ID: 5382024

NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {BLA 761344}
{Tarlatamab}
populations in monkeys administered ≥100 µg/kg or 4500 µg/kg in the 10-day and 1-month
studies, respectively; these changes also demonstrated reversibility. In a 1-month toxicology
study in mice administered muS757, a tarlatamab surrogate that binds mouse DLL3 and CD3,
decreased lymphocytes were also noted at ≥500 mg/kg muS757 and correlated with decreased
lymphoid cellularity in the lymph nodes. Additionally, decreased neutrophils were noted in
monkeys at 4500 µg/kg in the 1-month study and in mice administered ≥500 µg/kg muS757.
Other hematological changes, including decreased red blood cells, hemoglobin, and hematocrit
were noted at doses ≥100 µg/kg in the 10-day study in monkeys. The changes in red blood cell
parameters were not noted in the GLP studies of up to 3-months duration; however, anemia
was observed in humans. Consistent with nonclinical findings, decreases in lymphocytes and
neutropenia have occurred clinically and the IMDELLTRA (tarlatamab) label includes a warning
for neutropenia.
Signs of an inflammatory response were noted in the 10-day study, including increases in Creactive protein (CRP), MCP-1, and IFNγ at doses ≥100 µg/kg. Transient increases in heart rate
occurred in the 1-month monkey study at ≥500 µg/kg following the first dose, but this finding
was not noted at any time point in the 3-month study in monkeys. At doses ≥50 µg/kg, minimal
to mild mononuclear cell infiltrates were noted in the pituitary gland of monkeys; however, a
tissue-cross reactivity study using tissues from humans and monkeys demonstrated only
cytoplasmic DLL3 expression in pituitary and brain tissues, indicating DLL3 in these tissues is not
expected to be accessible to tarlatamab. Additionally, minimal to mild mononuclear and/or
mixed cell infiltrates were observed in multiple tissues in all toxicology studies in monkeys.
The Applicant did not conduct genetic toxicology studies as they generally are not appropriate
for antibody-based therapies. In addition, the Applicant did not conduct dedicated studies to
assess fertility. In general, these studies are not needed to support a marketing application for a
drug intended to treat patients with advanced cancer. There were no adverse or notable effects
on male or female reproductive organs in the repeat-dose toxicology studies conducted in
sexually mature cynomolgus monkeys.
Based on mechanism of action of tarlatamab, the expression of DLL3 during fetal development,
and data from a literature-based risk assessment, the pharmacology/toxicology team
recommends a warning for embryo-fetal toxicity in the label for IMDELLTRA. Tarlatamab causes
T cell-dependent depletion of DLL3-expressing cells, and T cell activation may result in cytokine
release and associated inflammatory effects that may adversely affect the ability to maintain a
pregnancy. Two administrations of a tarlatamab surrogate, mus757, that binds mouse CD3 and
DLL3, to pregnant mice on gestational days (GD) 6 and GD 13 did not result in maternal toxicity
or embryo-fetal development toxicity at doses ≤4500 µg/kg. muS757 was detected in fetal
serum at concentrations up to 30-times higher than maternal serum concentrations, indicating
the potential for tarlatamab to cross the placenta. Additionally, studies using genetically
engineered mice demonstrate that DLL3 loss-of-function mutations or deletion of DLL3
Version date: June 2022 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)

49

Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the
Applicant and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.

Reference ID: 5382024

NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {BLA 761344}
{Tarlatamab}
disrupted somitogenesis during embryogenesis leading to disorganization of the axial skeleton
and disruption of vertebral formation, suggesting that fetal exposure to tarlatamab may
increase the risk of abnormal skeletal development (Chapman et al., 2011, Dunwoodie et al.,
2002, Kusumi et al. 1998). In these studies, DLL3 was only detected intracellularly during
embryogenesis, which may explain the lack of findings in the embryo-fetal development study
in mice using muS757 given that tarlatamab is expected to target DLL3 expressed on the cell
surface.
Human immunoglobulin G (IgG) is known to cross the placenta via FcRn; tarlatamab can bind
FcRn, therefore, tarlatamab has the potential to be transmitted from the mother to the
developing fetus. Consistent with the recommendation for non-genotoxic drugs that are
teratogenic or embryo-fetal lethal as described in the FDA Guidance for Industry Oncology
Pharmaceuticals: Reproductive Toxicity Testing and Labeling Recommendations, and
considering the half-life of 11.2 days for tarlatamab, FDA recommends advising females of
reproductive potential to use effective contraception during treatment with IMDELLTRA and for
2 months after the last dose. The Applicant did not evaluate the presence of tarlatamab in milk;
however, maternal IgG is known to be present in human milk. The effects of local
gastrointestinal exposure and limited systemic exposure in the breastfed child to IMDELLTRA
are unknown. Because of the potential serious adverse effects of IMDELLTRA on a breastfed
child, the review team recommends advising patients not to breastfeed during treatment with
IMDELLTRA and for 2 months after the last dose.
Recommendation:
There are no outstanding issues from a pharmacology/toxicology perspective that would
prevent the approval of IMDELLTRA.

The Applicant’s Position:
Not applicable
The FDA’s Assessment:
The FDA agrees.

5.3. Pharmacology

Primary pharmacology

Tarlatamab (also designated as AMG 757) is a bispecific T-cell engager (BiTE®) molecule that
Version date: June 2022 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)

50

Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the
Applicant and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.

Reference ID: 5382024

NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {BLA 761344}
{Tarlatamab}
targets the tumor-associated antigen delta-like ligand 3 (DLL3). Tarlatamab consists of 2
single-chain variable fragment binding domains, specific for DLL3 and the T-cell receptor
(TCR)-associated protein cluster of differentiation 3 (CD3), fused to a silenced fragment
crystallizable (Fc) immunoglobulin domain to extend serum half-life. Tarlatamab is designed to
simultaneously bind to DLL3 that is expressed on the surface of cancer cells and CD3 expressed
on the surface of T cells.
The mechanism of action of tarlatamab is T cell-dependent lysis of tumor cells. The
pharmacological effect of tarlatamab is mediated by specific redirection of T cells to kill
DLL3-expressing tumor cells. The primary pharmacology of tarlatamab was assessed in SCLC cell
lines in vitro and in SCLC xenograft models in vivo.
Tarlatamab binding to human and cynomolgus monkey DLL3 was assessed by flow cytometry,
and binding to human and cynomolgus monkey CD3 was measured by surface plasmon
resonance (SPR) analysis (Table 3; Study Number: 122717/ electronic common technical
document [eCTD] Location: Module 4.2.1.1).
The binding of tarlatamab to Fc-gamma receptors (FcγR), the neonatal Fc receptor complex
(FcRn/B2M) and complement component 1q (C1q) was assessed by SPR analysis (Table 3; Study
Number: 157053/ eCTD Location: Module 4.2.1.1).
While the single-chain Fc region of tarlatamab showed low affinity binding to FcγRI/CD64, it did
not interact with FcγRIIa/CD32a and FcγRIIIa/CD16a. The interaction with FcγRI/CD64 was
characterized by very fast association and dissociation rates, and the equilibrium dissociation
constant (KD) value was about 7000-fold lower than that of a control monoclonal antibody,
rituximab (Study 157053). Tarlatamab did not bind to human C1q (Study 157053). Thus,
tarlatamab is not expected to mediate effector functions such as antibody-dependent cell
mediated cytotoxicity and complement-dependent cytotoxicity. Furthermore, tarlatamab
showed a pH-dependent interaction with human FcRn/B2M heterodimer receptors with a KD
value of 444 nM, which was comparable to rituximab (345 nM) (Study 157053).

Tarlatamab engaged T cells from human or cynomolgus monkey to effectively lyse SCLC cells in
vitro (Table 4; Study Number: 122717/ eCTD Location: Module 4.2.1.1). The cross-reactivity of
tarlatamab to cynomolgus monkey shown by similar binding affinity and cytotoxic activity
supported selection of cynomolgus monkey for nonclinical toxicology studies.

Bispecific binding of tarlatamab to DLL3 on tumor cells and the TCR/CD3 complex on T cells
induces similar effects as antigen-specific recognition of peptide-major histocompatibility
complex by the TCR of T cells. Thus, tarlatamab initiates activity of cytotoxic T lymphocytes
through the upregulation of key molecules of T-cell activation and transient cytokine
production by T cells (Study 122717).
In co-cultures of DLL3-expressing SHP-77 and human T cells, tarlatamab-induced
dose-dependent upregulation of T-cell activation markers, including CD69 (Figure 1; Study
Number: 122717/ eCTD Location: Module 4.2.1.1).

Version date: June 2022 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)

52

Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the
Applicant and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.

Reference ID: 5382024

NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {BLA 761344}
{Tarlatamab}
In vivo, tarlatamab activity was evaluated in xenograft models of SCLC. The SHP-77 and NCI-H82
cell lines that had been characterized in the in vitro studies were used in orthotopic xenograft
models. The SHP-77 cells primarily formed tumors in the lung to mimic primary SCLC tumors,
and the NCI-H82 cells formed tumor metastases predominantly in liver, characteristic of typical
SCLC metastasis. In addition, 2 patient-derived xenograft (PDX) tumor models that were used,
LXFS 1129 and LXFS 538, were representative of the DLL3 expression levels and tumor
heterogeneity observed in human SCLC tumors (Giffin et al, 2021).
The tarlatamab mechanism of action requires the simultaneous binding to DLL3 on tumor cells
and CD3 on T cells. As tarlatamab does not cross-react to mouse CD3, the xenograft models
used in the nonclinical pharmacology studies were conducted in immunocompromised mice
(non-obese diabetic/severe combined immunodeficiency, interleukin 2 receptor gamma [NSG])
that were injected with activated, expanded human T cells before treatment with tarlatamab.
Together, the xenograft models enabled direct testing of the clinical molecule tarlatamab and
facilitated evaluation of pharmacodynamic activity in tumors (T-cell infiltration and/or
expansion, T-cell activation, and tumor cell apoptosis) and antitumor activity.

{Tarlatamab}
The FDA’s Assessment:
The FDA generally agrees with the Applicant’s position. In addition to these findings, the FDA
notes the following results:
At a concentration of 50 ng/mL, tarlatamab bound human and cynomolgus T cells, but did not
bind T cells from mice, rats, rabbits, dogs, or minipigs (Study# R20140017). Binding to human
and monkey CD3 and DLL3 was further characterized in in vitro assays. Tarlatamab bound CHO
cells expressing human or monkey DLL3 with KD values of 0.64 nM and 0.5 nM, respectively;
tarlatamab did not bind untransfected CHO cells (Study# 122717). As assessed by SPR,
tarlatamab bound human CD3 with a KD of 14.9 nM and monkey CD3 with a KD of 12 nM.
Additionally, tarlatamab demonstrated similar binding to human and monkey FcRn in a
qualitative SPR assay (Study# 122717). Given that tarlatamab demonstrated the ability to bind
monkey CD3 and DLL3 similarly to human CD3 and DLL3, the monkey was selected for toxicity
testing of tarlatamab.
Tarlatamab mediated lysis of multiple human small cell lung cancer cell lines endogenously
expressing DLL3 on the cell surface by human and monkey PBMCs but did not mediate lysis of
HCT-116 human colon cancer cells that do not express DLL3 (Study # 122717). Cytotoxicity was
also dependent on the expression level of DLL3 on target cells as demonstrated by findings of
lower EC50 values in assays with target cells expressing high levels of DLL3 (SHP-77, EC50=32 pM)
compared to assays using target cells with intermediate (COR-L279, EC50 = 57 pm) or low levels
of DLL3 (H-2171, EC50=161 pM).
Tarlatamab-mediated T cell lysis of DLL3+ target cells was time-dependent with cytotoxicity
initially observed after 16 hours of culture and maximum cytotoxicity observed after 48 hours
of culture. Though maximum cytotoxicity increased over time, EC50 values remained consistent
across the incubation period with EC50 values of 23.2 pM to 41.4 pM for co-cultures with DMS
79 cells and EC50 values of 5.5 to 7.7 pM for co-cultures with SHP-77 cells (Study # 122717).
The effect of donor variability on in vitro T cell mediated lysis was assessed by co-culturing
human PBMCs (n=12) with DLL3+ SHP-77 target cells at an effector-to target cell ratio (E:T) of
5:1 for 48 hours, then evaluating target cell viability by flow cytometry. PBMCs from all donors
mediated cytotoxicity of SHP-77 cells with an average EC50 of 26.4 pM and maximum
cytotoxicity of 74% to 86% (Study # 122717).
The ability of tarlatamab to mediate lysis of DLL3+ target cell by CD4 or CD8 T cells was
evaluated in an in vitro assay (Study# R20140017). CD4 and CD8 T cells were enriched from
human PBMCs and co-cultured with SHP-77 cells at an E:T of 5:1 for up to 72 hours, then
cytotoxicity was assessed by flow cytometry. Though maximal cytotoxicity was similar after 24
to 72 hours of co-culture in assays using CD4 or CD8 T cells, EC50 values for CD4-mediated lysis
Version date: June 2022 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)

57

Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the
Applicant and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.

Reference ID: 5382024

NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {BLA 761344}
{Tarlatamab}
(EC50=29.7 pM to 47.8 pM) were slightly higher than EC50 values for CD8-mediated lysis
(EC50=3.8 to 16.1 pm). These results indicate that both CD4 and CD8 T cells contribute to
tarlatamab-mediated lysis of DLL3+ target cells by T cells. Similar findings were also observed in
cultures using CD4 and CD8 T cells from monkey PBMCs (Study # 122717).
In the presence of DLL3-expressing SHP-77 cells, treatment with tarlatamab resulted in
increased expression of CD71, PD-1, and PD-L1, in addition to CD69 expression on CD4 and CD8
T cells (Study # 122717). These findings further indicate the ability of tarlatamab to activate CD4
and CD8 T cells.
In a separate assay, treatment with tarlatamab resulted in upregulation of activation markers
CD25 and CD69 on human T cells when PBMCs were cultured alone or in the presence of HCT116 cells lacking expression of DLL3, suggesting tarlatamab may mediate T cell activation
independently of DLL3 engagement; however, upregulation of CD25 and CD69 were abolished
when physiological levels of human IgG were added to the assay conditions (Study # 122717).
These findings suggest that low affinity binding of the ScFc region of tarlatamab to FcγRI on
target cells and simultaneous binding to CD3 on T cells can induce T cell activation but
competitive binding from human IgG can block tarlatamab binding to FcγRs, thereby inhibiting
target-independent T cell activation. These findings were further confirmed in an assay using an
anti-CD64 blocking antibody (Study # 122717).
Dose dependent cytokine release was also observed in co-cultures with H-2171, NCI-H889, and
SHP-77 human SCLC cell lines (Study # 122717). Higher maximal cytokine release was noted in
co-cultures with SHP-77 cells endogenously expressing high levels of DLL3 compared to H-2171
cells expressing comparatively lower levels of DLL3, further suggesting that tarlatamab activity
is dependent upon expression levels of DLL3 on target cells (Study # 122717). Cytokine release
was also time-dependent with maximal release of IL-2, IL-4, IL-10, TNF-α, and IFNγ occurring
after approximately 16 hours of culture, then decreasing by 24 hours; however, IFNγ levels
remained elevated through 72 hours of culture. Comparatively, IL-6 release peaked after 48
hours of cultures.
In addition to increased expression of activation markers in co-cultures with DLL3+ target cells,
tarlatamab resulted in increased expression of granzyme B and perforin, markers of cytotoxic
activity on T cells (Study # 122717). Human PBMCs were co-cultured with either SHP-77 or DMS
79 human SCLC cells at an E:T of 2:1 in the presence of tarlatamab for up to 72 hours, then
expression of perforin and granzyme B were assessed by flow cytometry. Up to 50% of CD8 T
cells were positive for perforin in the presence of tarlatamab, though only a minor percentage
of CD4 T cells expressed perforin after co-culture with DLL3-expressing target cells in the
presence of tarlatamab. Tarlatamab also resulted in increased expression of granzyme B in CD4
and CD8 T cells in a concentration-dependent manner with similar maximal increases in both T
cell populations (Study # 122717).
Version date: June 2022 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)

58

Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the
Applicant and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.

Reference ID: 5382024

NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {BLA 761344}
{Tarlatamab}
Dexamethasone may be used clinically to antagonize severe inflammatory responses. To
evaluate the effect of dexamethasone on the activity of tarlatamab, human PBMCs were cocultured with DMS 79 or SHP-77 human small cell lung cancer cell lines in the presence of
tarlatamab and dexamethasone, then T cell activation and target cell cytotoxicity were assessed
by flow cytometry and supernatants were collected to evaluate cytokine release. Results were
compared to co-cultures treated with tarlatamab but not dexamethasone. Though tarlatamab
resulted in concentration-dependent increased expression of the T cell activation markers CD69
and CD25 in cultures treated with and without dexamethasone, maximal responses were lower
and EC50 values were approximately 3.3- and 3.7-times higher for co-cultures with DMS 79 and
SHP-77 cells, respectively. Treatment with dexamethasone also resulted in lower release of
IFNγ, TNF-α, IL-10, and IL-6. These findings suggest dexamethasone may reduce tarlatamabmediated T cell activation in the presence of DLL3+ target cells (Study# 122718).
As described by the Applicant above, tarlatamab inhibited growth of multiple DLL3-expressing
tumors in mice when administered intraperitoneally at doses ≥0.1 mg/kg. Anti-tumor activity of
tarlatamab was associated with expansion of tumor-infiltrating CD4 and CD8 T cells in mice
bearing SHP-77 or NCI-H82 SCLC tumors compared to mice treated with an isotype control
(Study#s R201330093 and R20190094).
Secondary Pharmacology
The Applicant’s Position:
The DLL3 expression profile was analyzed in normal human and cynomolgus monkey tissues:
•

DLL3 transcript variants exist that do not contain the sequences recognized by tarlatamab
(Study Numbers: 124207; 124208/ eCTD Location: Module 4.2.1.2).

•

The quantification of canonical DLL3 transcripts that contain the tarlatamab binding region
indicates extremely low levels of expression in a small subset of normal tissues (Study
Numbers: 122642; 123658; 124208; / eCTD Location: Module 4.2.1.2).

•

The DLL3 protein expression is extremely low in this limited subset of tissues (Study
Numbers: 123377 and 123293/ eCTD Location: Module 4.2.1.2).

•

The expression pattern appears to be restricted to the cytoplasm although membranous
expression below the limit of detection might be possible (Study Number: 123377/ eCTD
Location: Module 4.2.1.2).

Therefore, it is anticipated that tarlatamab administration is not associated with secondary
pharmacodynamics leading to normal tissue damage since DLL3 in the cytoplasm would not be
accessible to tarlatamab.

To evaluate the potential for off-target activation of platelets, tarlatamab was tested in a flow
cytometry assay for platelet activation at concentrations of 125, 41.7, 13.9, 4.6, 1.5, and
0.5 µg/mL in both human and cynomolgus monkey whole blood. No activation of platelets was
observed in either human or cynomolgus monkey whole blood (Study Number 122111 / eCTD
Location: Module 4.2.1.2).
The FDA’s Assessment:
The FDA generally agrees with the Applicant’s position. Additional findings include:
Non-canonical DLL3 mRNA transcript variants were identified in normal human and monkey
tissues, but these transcripts do not contain sequences coding for tarlatamab binding regions of
DLL3 (Study# 123658, 124207). DLL3 mRNA transcripts for canonical, full length DLL3 were
identified at low levels in in the optic nerve of monkeys and the brains of humans and monkeys
(Study#s 124208, 123658, 122642).
As assessed by immunohistochemistry, cytoplasmic DLL3 staining was noted in the pituitary,
pancreatic islets, and in neurons in several regions of the brain (cerebrum, cerebellum,
hypothalamus, hippocampus), though staining was noted as generally weak to occasionally mild
in intensity (Study# 123377). These findings suggest that though DLL3 may be expressed in
some tissues, DLL3 is retained intracellularly and would not be expected to be accessible for
tarlatamab.
Safety Pharmacology
Consistent with the International Council for Harmonisation (ICH) of Technical Requirements for
Pharmaceuticals for Human Use Guideline: Nonclinical Evaluation for Anticancer
Pharmaceuticals S9 (ICH, 2010) no stand-alone safety pharmacology studies were conducted for
tarlatamab. Electrocardiographic assessments, respiratory rate, body temperature
measurements, and neurologic examinations were included on the toxicology studies in the
cynomolgus monkey (Section 5.5.1 / Study Numbers 121423; 121424; 122516 / eCTD location:
Module 4.2.3.2).
The FDA’s Assessment:
The FDA agrees.

5.4. ADME/PK

The Applicant’s Position:

Version date: June 2022 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)

60

Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the
Applicant and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.

Reference ID: 5382024

NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {BLA 761344}
{Tarlatamab}
upregulated in neuroendocrine tumor cells and localizes to the tumor cell surface. This
differential expression profile makes DLL3 a compelling, tumor-selective therapeutic target.
Therefore, tarlatamab-associated changes observed in the toxicology studies were limited and
are consistent with the expected mechanism of action given that DLL3 has a limited tissue
distribution combined with low levels of intracellular expression.
The cynomolgus monkey was selected as species for nonclinical safety evaluation of tarlatamab
based on target binding affinity and bioactivity data. The IV route of administration was chosen
for in vivo nonclinical studies based on the intended clinical route of administration. Due to the
physicochemical properties of tarlatamab, the maximal feasible dose that could be
administered to cynomolgus monkey was limited to 4500 µg/kg.
The nonclinical safety assessment of tarlatamab was based on a 10-day exploratory as well as
28-day GLP and 3-month GLP repeat-dose IV toxicology studies with once weekly doses of up to
4500 µg/kg.
In the 10-day exploratory toxicology study with dosages of ≤ 1000 µg/kg, tarlatamab-related
effects were limited to a body temperature increase in a single high-dose animal on day 10,
decreases in lymphocyte populations, minimal increases in C-reactive protein (CRP), monocyte
chemoattractant protein-1 (MCP-1) and IFNγ serum concentrations. Tarlatamab-related light
microscopy findings were limited to minimal and mild mixed inflammatory cell infiltration in
lung, liver, and pituitary gland with no indication of cellular damage in any tissue.
In the 28-day study with dosages of ≤ 4500 µg/kg, tarlatamab-related changes consistent with
redirected cell lysis were limited to a minor decrease in lymphocyte populations (detected after
the second dose only) at 4500 ≤µg/kg. The only other tarlatamab-related effects were a slightly
increased heart rate (on day 2 but not at day 23) at ≥ 500 µg/kg as well as a minimal to mild
mixed cell infiltrate comprised of lymphocytes and eosinophils in the pituitary at 50 µg/kg and
500 µg/kg which showed evidence of reversibility by the end of the 28-day recovery period. A
dose-related increase in incidence of antidrug antibodies (ADAs) occurred, which potentially
contributed to variability in exposure levels at the end of the study. One animal had vascular
injury-associated changes in the heart and lung that were consistent with secondary effects
likely mediated by ADA-related immune complexes.
In the 3-month study, tarlatamab-related clinical pathology changes were limited to a minimal
decrease in lymphocytes at 4500 µg/kg, the maximum dose administered. In addition,
tarlatamab-related changes were observed in the pituitary gland at ≥ 50 µg/kg, characterized as
a minimal to mild mononuclear cell infiltrate composed primarily of lymphocytes with no
evidence of tissue injury.
Local tolerance of IV administration of tarlatamab was evaluated in the 28-day and the 3-month
toxicology studies in the cynomolgus monkey. In the 28-day as well as the 3-month study, no
evidence of irritation or other local tarlatamab-related effects were observed at the IV
administration sites.

Tarlatamab-related changes are reversible and clinically monitorable, and the highest
non-severely toxic dose was determined to be 4500 µg/kg based on the absence of any
tarlatamab-related adverse findings.
Following the proposal of the FDA, a murine surrogate molecule of tarlatamab, designated
muS757, was developed to evaluate potential effects of a DLL3-targeting BiTE molecule on
embryofetal development in mice.

The FDA’s Assessment:
The FDA generally agrees with the Applicant’s summaries of the repeat-dose toxicology studies
in monkeys. Though the monkey was chosen for toxicity testing based on the ability of
tarlatamab to bind human and monkey CD3 and DLL3 similarly in in vitro assays, toxicity
associated with tarlatamab may be underrepresented in the monkey given that
immunohistochemistry data described above indicates that DLL3 is expressed intracellularly in
normal tissues and cells.
In addition to decreases in lymphocytes that were noted in all toxicology studies in monkeys,
increases in large unstained cells (up to 213%) and decreases in neutrophils (up to -52%) were
noted at ≥50 µg/kg in the 1-month study and decreased eosinophils (up to -76%) were noted at

Though interpretation of findings from the 10-day study in monkeys is limited by the study
design, including lack of a vehicle-treated control and limited number of animals (n=2
females/group), findings suggestive of a systemic inflammatory response were noted in
monkeys administered 1000 µg/kg, including transient increases in CRP (300%) and neutrophils
(468%) that occurred 4 to 24 hours after the first dose compared to baseline values, as well as
histological signs of minimal to mild mixed cell infiltrates in the liver, lung, and pituitary gland.
Changes in lymphocyte populations in monkeys correlated with decreased CD3, CD4, and CD8 T
cell populations following the first or second dose of tarlatamab at ≥100 µg/kg in the 10-day
study and at 4500 µg/kg in the 1-month study; decreases in T cell populations improved by the
end of the dosing phase in the 1-month monkey study. Decreases in B cells and NK cells also
occurred at ≥100 µg/kg in the 10-day monkey study.
Similar findings of decreased leukocytes, lymphocytes, and neutrophils were also noted in mice
administered ≥500 µg/kg of a mouse tarlatamab surrogate, muS757, and correlated with
decreased lymphoid cellularity in the lymph nodes at 4500 µg/kg.
Though signs of anemia were not observed in the 1-month or 3-month studies in monkeys
administered tarlatamab or the 1-month study in mice with a mouse surrogate of tarlatamab,
hematologic changes noted in the 10-Day study in monkeys included decreases in red blood
cells (up to -21%), hemoglobin (up to -20%), and hematocrit (up to -21%) and increased
reticulocytes (up to 447%) in animals administered ≥100 µg/kg compared to pre-dose values.
These changes occurred 4 hours after the first dose and persisted through the dosing phase.
A transient increase in AST (up to 450% compared to pre-dose values) occurred at 1000 µg/kg
in the 10-day study following the first dose but improved by the end of the dosing phase. The
relevance of this finding is unclear as there was no histologic correlate and liver changes were
not noted at doses ≤4500 µg/kg in the 1-month or 3-month monkey studies.
Though transient increases in heart rate were observed at doses ≥500 µg/kg in the 1-month
toxicology study, this finding was only noted in 2 male monkeys on Day 2 and increased heart
rate or other ECG changes were not noted in the 3-month monkey study.
Additional findings noted in the 3-month monkey study at doses ≥50 µg/kg include abnormal
feces color and decreased thyroid weight, though the relevance of these findings is unclear as
no correlates were identified and they only occurred in females.

5.5.2.

Genetic Toxicology

The Applicant’s Position:
No studies evaluating the genotoxic or mutagenic potential of tarlatamab have been
conducted. Tarlatamab is a recombinant BiTE molecule made up entirely of naturally-occurring
amino acids and contains no inorganic or synthetic organic linkers or other non-protein
portions. Therefore, no genotoxicity studies have been conducted or are planned, consistent
with ICH Harmonised Tripartite Guideline S6, “Preclinical Safety Evaluation of BiotechnologyDerived Pharmaceuticals” (ICH S6[R1], 2011).
The FDA’s Assessment:
The FDA agrees.
5.5.3.

Carcinogenicity

The Applicant’s Position:
No carcinogenicity studies have been conducted nor are any planned in accordance with ICH S9
(ICH, 2010).
The FDA’s Assessment:
The FDA agrees.

5.5.4.

Reproductive and Developmental Toxicology

Following the proposal of the FDA, a murine surrogate molecule of tarlatamab, designated
muS757, was developed to evaluate potential effects of a DLL3-targeting BiTE molecule on
embryofetal development in mice. The study objectives were to detect potential adverse
effects of muS757 on pregnant Crl:CD1 female mice and development of the embryo and fetus
consequent to exposure of the female from implantation to closure of the hard palate (Study
Number 122517/ eCTD location: Module 4.2.3.5.2).
Timed-mated female mice (22/group) received the vehicle control, 500 or 4500 mg/kg muS757
via IV bolus injection on gestation days (GD) 6 and 13. There were no muS757-related effects on
any maternal parameters, including mean maternal body weights or body weight gains. In

The FDA’s Assessment:
The FDA generally agrees with the Applicant’s conclusion and acknowledges that muS757 (a
tarlatamab surrogate that binds mouse CD3 and DLL3) crossed the placental barrier.
Additionally, there were no signs of toxicity to male or female reproductive organs in the
general toxicology studies in monkeys administered repeat doses of tarlatamab.
Tarlatamab is a bispecific T cell engager and causes T cell-dependent depletion of DLL3expressing cells. Risks associated with T cell activation include cytokine release and associated
inflammatory effects. The occurrence of inflammatory adverse events in the mother may
adversely affect the pregnancy.
Studies using genetically engineered mice indicate DLL3 plays roles in skeletal development
during embryogenesis. DLL3 was found to be expressed intracellularly in the presomitic
mesoderm of mouse embryos and to contribute to somitogenesis, a process leading to
segmentation in animals that ultimately gives rise to skeletal, muscle, and other connective
76
Version date: June 2022 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)
Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the
Applicant and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.

Reference ID: 5382024

NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {BLA 761344}
{Tarlatamab}
tissues (Chapman et al., 2011). Additional studies have identified DLL3 mRNA transcripts
present in unfertilized oocytes and persisting through gastrulation and early organogenesis
(Cormier et al., 2004; Dunwoodie et al., 1997). DLL3 loss-of function mutations or deletion of
DLL3 in mice leads to delayed and irregular somite formation/segmentation, which results in a
phenotype characterized by a disorganized axial skeleton and disruption of vertebral formation,
including hemivertebrae associated with disorganized neural arches and rib bifurcations
(Dunwoodie et al., 2002, Kusumi et al., 1998)
Further, genetic mutations in DLL3 in humans have been associated with spondylocostal
dysostosis, a congenital condition causing segmentation defects of the vertebrae and
abnormalities of the ribs that result in a shortened trunk and/or neck (Chapman et al., 2011).
Together, these findings suggest that fetal exposure to tarlatamab may increase the risk of
abnormal skeletal development.
Given that only DLL3 expressed on the cell surface is expected to be a target for tarlatamab, the
intracellular expression pattern of DLL3 noted during fetal development may explain the lack of
findings in the EFD study in mice using a mouse tarlatamab surrogate. Though DLL3 has been
detected intracellularly during embryogenesis, it remains unknown whether DLL3 is displayed
at the cell surface at other timepoints during embryofetal development.
In humans and mice, transfer of IgG antibodies from mother to fetus across the placenta is
mediated by the neonatal Fc receptor (FcRn). Tarlatamab can bind the FcRn and may cross the
placenta to the developing fetus similarly as an endogenous IgG antibody. Fetal serum
concentrations of muS757 were approximately 30-times higher than maternal serum
concentrations, indicating potential for tarlatamab to cross the placenta. Additionally,
tarlatamab is based on an IgG antibody and may be present in milk; a breastfed child may be
exposed to tarlatamab via lactational transfer.
Based on the mechanism of action of tarlatamab, FDA recommends including a Warning for
embryo-fetal toxicity and advising females of reproductive potential to use effective
contraception during treatment with tarlatamab and for 2 months after the last dose.

The Applicant’s Position:
No other toxicology studies using tarlatamab or muS757 were conducted.

6 Clinical Pharmacology

6.1. Executive Summary

The FDA’s Assessment:

The Applicant submitted current BLA 761344 for the proposed indication of tarlatamab
monotherapy for the treatment of adult patients with advanced small cell lung cancer
(SCLC) with disease progression on or after platinum-based chemotherapy. The
proposed dosage of tarlatamab is 1 mg on Cycle 1 day 1, followed by 10 mg on Cycle 1
days 8 and 15, and every 2 weeks (Q2W) onward (hereafter referred to as 1/10 mg)
administered via a 1-hour intravenous (IV) infusion.
Study 20200491 provides the primary efficacy evidence to support the proposed
indication. Part 1 of this trial is a dose randomization study which showed the overall
response rate (ORR) of 40% (95% CI: 31%, 51%) in 1/10 mg cohort and 32% (95% CI:
23%, 41%) in 1/100 mg (at the same dosing schedule as 1/10 mg) with 1/10 mg cohort
demonstrated a numerically lower rate of any grade neutropenia and Grade 1 and
Grade 2 neurotoxicity including immune effector cell-associated neurotoxicity syndrome
(ICANS), supporting the selection of 1/10 mg dosage for Part 2 and Part 3 of Study
20200491.
The key review issues are focused on the evaluation of dose selection, drug interaction
management and recommendations for restarting therapy after dose delay. The
population pharmacokinetics (popPK) analyses found none of the intrinsic or extrinsic
covariates including age (32 to 82 years), bodyweight (35 to 149 kg), sex, race (White
and Asian), mild or moderate renal impairment, or mild hepatic impairment had a
clinically important effect on the pharmacokinetic (PK) of tarlatamab. The exposureresponse (E-R) analyses found a positive trend between ORR and exposure with a
plateau being achieved at 1/10 mg. A statistically significant positive E-R for safety was
also identified for all grade neutropenia and Grade 1 and Grade 2 neurotoxicity
including ICANS. The impact of anti-drug antibodies (ADAs) on efficacy, safety, and PK of
tarlatamab is unknown due to a low ADA incidence of 3%. Transient elevations of
circulating cytokines interleukin (IL)2, IL-6, IL-8, IL-10, and interferon‐gamma (INF ‐γ)
were observed at dosages starting at 0.3 mg. The integrated popPK, E-R,
pharmacodynamics (PD) analyses along with safety, and efficacy from the Study
20200491 and the dose-finding first in human (FIH) study 20160323 support the
proposed tarlatamab 1/10 mg dosing regimen.
Recommendations
Version date: June 2022 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)

79

Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the
Applicant and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.

Reference ID: 5382024

NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {BLA 761344}
{Tarlatamab}
developed neutralizing antibodies. Potential impact of antitarlatamab antibodies on the
PK, clinical efficacy, and clinical safety of tarlatamab was assessed with the following
results:
•

Based on the population PK analysis, individuals with positive antitarlatamab
antibody binding status at any time during the study were estimated to have
approximately 21% higher clearance, which translated to an approximately 24%
decrease in serum trough concentrations on average. However, this difference in
exposures did not have a clinically relevant impact on efficacy.

•

At the proposed dosing regimen, the efficacy measures (including ORR, disease
control rate [DCR], and DOR) were similar for antitarlatamab binding antibody
positive subjects compared with those who were antitarlatamab antibody negative.

•

There was no apparent impact of antitarlatamab antibody development on safety
including adverse events.

A detailed description of these analyses can be found in the Integrated Summary of
Immunogenicity (Module 5.3.5.3).
The Applicant’s Position:
In both Studies 20160232 and 20200491, tarlatamab exhibited approximate
dose-proportional increases in exposures in the evaluated dose range. The steady state
in tarlatamab serum concentrations were approximately achieved by the start of cycle 2.
The mean (SD) estimated terminal phase elimination half-life at steady state was 5.5
(1.6) days, across the evaluated dose range. The incidence of antitarlatamab antibody
development was low. There was no meaningful clinical impact of antitarlatamab
antibody development on efficacy or safety.
The FDA’s Assessment:
The clinical pharmacology program included data from Studies 20160232 and 20200491.
FDA agrees that the results of these studies generally provide adequate characterization
of tarlatamab PK properties as described below and the PK parameters are presented as
geometric mean (CV%) unless otherwise specified:
Dose proportionality: The exposure of tarlatamab increased dose proportionally in
the evaluated dose range of 1 mg to 100 mg.
Distribution: Tarlatamab’s steady state volume of distribution is 8.6 L (18.3%).
Elimination: Tarlatamab’s median elimination half-life (min, max) is 11.2 (4.3 to 26.5)
days, and the estimated clearance is 0.65 L/day (44%) in patients with SCLC. Steady
state was achieved by Cycle 2 Day 15.
Immunogenicity: 3 % (4/214) of patients tested positive for ADA and none of the
patients tested positive for nAb in the pivotal Study 20200491.
Refer to section 6.3.1 and 19.4.1 for details.

6.2.2.

General Dosing and Therapeutic Individualization

6.2.2.1.

General Dosing

Data:
The proposed dose and dosing regimen for tarlatamab for the treatment of previously
treated advanced SCLC is an initial 1 mg dose on cycle 1 day 1, followed by 10 mg on
cycle 1 day 8, cycle 1 day 15, and Q2W thereafter, all doses administered via a 1-hour IV
infusion. The totality of exposure-response data from the dose exploration and
expansion parts of the phase 1 FIH Study 20160323 (dose range 0.003 to 100 mg Q2W
and 200 mg Q3W) supported the selection of 2 dose levels of 10 and 100 mg Q2W for
further assessment in a randomized manner in Part 1 of the pivotal phase 2
Study 20200491. The analyses from the FIH Study 20160323 demonstrated numerically
higher efficacy to be associated with 10 mg exposures relative to lower doses
(1 or 3 mg). The analysis also displayed numerical benefit in DCR with exposures
associated with the 100 mg Q2W regimen relative to the 10 mg Q2W regimen, therefore
supporting the selection of 100 mg as the higher dose level (in addition to 10 mg) to be
evaluated in pivotal Study 20200491. Importantly, from a safety and tolerability
perspective, a maximal tolerated dose level was not achieved in the ongoing FIH
Study 20160323, within the evaluated dose range.
Of the 2 dose levels assessed in Part 1 of pivotal Study 20200491, the 10 mg Q2W
regimen was selected at the prespecified interim analysis for further evaluation in
Part 2. At the time of interim analysis, measures of efficacy response were similar
between the 10 and 100 mg groups. In the 10 mg group, the confirmed ORR (assessed
by the investigator) was 34.4% (95% CI: 18.6, 53.2) and the DCR was 75.0% (95% CI:
56.6, 88.5). In the 100 mg group, the confirmed ORR was 35.5% (95% CI: 19.2, 54.6), and
the DCR was 64.5% (95% CI: 45.4, 80.8) (Table 12 of Rationale for Recommended Phase
2 Dose [RP2D] (Module 5.3.5.2). At the time of the interim analysis, compared with the
100 mg group, subjects in the 10 mg group experienced fewer treatment-emergent
adverse events (Table 19 of Rationale for RP2D). Consistent with the dose-response
data, exposure-response analyses demonstrated that exposures with the 10 mg dose
regimen were associated with improved tolerability but comparable efficacy relative to
the 100 mg dose. Further details of the data available and analysis results at the time of
prespecified interim analysis are provided in the Rationale for RP2D (Module 5.3.5.2).
To confirm the selection of 10 mg Q2W regimen, these exposure-response analyses
were updated using pooled data from Study 20160323 and Study 20200491 based on
the latest cutoff dates of 28 March 2023 and 27 June 2023, respectively. These analyses
further confirmed that the proposed dosing regimen of 10 mg Q2W exhibited near
maximal efficacy and favorable benefit-risk profile in the intended patient population.
The Applicant’s Position:
Version date: June 2022 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)

Overall, results of exposure-response analysis suggest the proposed dosing regimen of
10 mg Q2W exhibited near maximal efficacy with a limited impact on neutropenia and
favorable benefit-risk profile in the intended patient population. None of the covariates
evaluated in this analysis resulted in a clinically meaningful change in efficacy or safety
and therefore do not warrant a dose adjustment.
The FDA’s Assessment:
FDA agrees with the Applicant’s position on dose selection of 1/10 mg based on efficacy
and safety data from Studies 20200491 and 20160232.
The proposed 1 mg step-dose regimen appears to be acceptable based on the following
assessment:
The risk of CRS of an alternative step-up dose for 10 mg target doses is unknown as no
alternative step-up regimen was investigated. In Study 20160232, 1 mg step-up was
investigated for all target dosages starting at 3 mg. The risk of Grade 2 or above CRS
occurrence appeared to be low (≤ 30 %) at all dosages (Table 14). The 1 mg step-up was
further evaluated in Study 20200491.
Following administration of 1/10 mg in Study 20200491, in the primary safety
population, the majority of CRS incidences were Grade 1 (30%), with the highest rate
(40%) observed following the step-up dose 1 mg on Cycle 1 Day 1 (
Table 15 and Figure 3). Grade 3 CRS occurred less frequently in the 1/10 mg cohort (1%)
than in the 1/100-mg cohort (6%). The risk of CRS in the subsequent cycles of the 10 mg
target doses was mitigated by the initial 1 mg step-up dose. Majority of CRS had a fast
onset of 11 to 25 hours, subsequent to 1 mg on Cycle 1 Day 1 and 10 mg on Cycle 1 Days
8. According to the Sunburst plot (Figure 5), incidences of late onset CRS were low.
Thirteen patients (9%) who did not experience CRS following the step-up dose 1 mg,
developed CRS following the first treatment dose 10 mg on Cycle 1 Day 8; one patient
(1%) developed CRS following the second treatment dose on Cycle 1 Day 15 among the
patients who did not experience CRS after step-up dose and first treatment dose. For
1/10 mg cohort, a few patients’ treatment discontinuation(n=3) and dose interruption
(n=4) events might be attributable to CRS occurrence and labeled as no dose events.
Other no dose events in Figure 5 and Figure 3 were attributable to other AEs, patient
withdrawal and disease progression. Overall, there is no significant CRS concern
identified with the 1 mg step-up dose for the 10 mg treatment doses.
In Study 20200491, 1/100 mg dosage resulted in a higher overall CRS rate of 63% and a
different CRS dynamics with the highest CRS (49%) including Grade 2 (13%) and Grade 3
(5%) observed following the 100 mg treatment dose on Cycle 1 Day 8 (Figure 4 and

FDA agrees with Applicant’s selection of 10 mg treatment doses based on the following
assessments:
1)

The efficacy data from Study 20160323 indicated that dosages ≥ 3 mg
are active (Table 14). Based on the predicted higher antitumor activities
and acceptable safety, 1/10 mg and 1/100 mg were selected to expand
in Part 1 of Study 20200491 dose randomization study. The 1/10 mg
cohorts achieved a numerically higher confirmed ORR than 1/100 mg
cohort with fewer all grade neutropenia and ICANs. A benefit-risk
assessment based on results from Study 20200491 determined that 1/10
mg achieved a clinically meaningful efficacy with an acceptable safety
profile. Refer to section 8.1.7 and 8.2 for detail clinical efficacy and
safety summary.

2)

FDA agrees that the pertinent E-R analyses appear to be adequate with
sufficient number of patients (N = 217) from 1/10 and 1/100 mg cohorts.
