completed by the Applicant and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.
Reference ID: 5382024

NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {BLA 761344}
{Tarlatamab}
mean (SD) estimated terminal phase elimination half-life at steady state was 5.5 (1.6)
days across the evaluated dose range.
Following the proposed dosing regimen of 1 mg on day 1 and 10 mg on day 8, day 15,
and Q2W thereafter, tarlatamab geometric mean trough values at steady state (cycle 2
day 15 predose) and peak serum concentrations at steady state (cycle 2 day 15, end of
infusion) were 0.44 and 4.3 mg/mL, respectively, based on pooled data across phase 1
and phase 2 studies. There was no indication of time dependent kinetics.
Based on population PK analyses, tarlatamab serum clearance, central volume of
distribution, peripheral volume of distribution, and volume of distribution at steady
state were estimated to be 0.65 L/day, 3.44 L, 5.06 L, and 8.5 L, respectively for a typical
73 kg White individual. The inter-individual variability for tarlatamab clearance and
central volume of distribution were 43.7% and 38.7%, respectively.
Various demographic variables were evaluated in the covariate testing within the
population PK analyses framework. Key demographic factors (age, sex, ethnicity),
estimates of renal function and liver function, number of prior lines of therapies,
baseline disease burden or ECOG status, did not affect tarlatamab PK.
Consistent with other biologics, body weight was correlated with tarlatamab clearance,
as was positive binding ADA status with increase in body weight or positive ADA binding
status associated with increase in clearance. However, at the proposed dosing regimen
of 1 mg on day 1 followed by 10 mg on day 8, day 15, and Q2W thereafter, neither body
weight nor ADA status had a clinically relevant impact on tarlatamab efficacy as
assessed by confirmed ORR or DCR. Race was found to be correlated with central
volume of distribution; however, race had a negligible impact on exposures.
At the proposed dosing regimen of 10 mg Q2W, the incidence of treatment-emergent
antitarlatamab antibodies was 12% (3 of 25 subjects) for the FIH Study 20160323 and
3.2% (4 of 124 subjects) for the pivotal phase 2 Study 20200491, for a pooled total of 7
of 149 subjects (4.7%) across the 2 studies. In the pivotal phase 2 Study 20200491,
which employed the neutralizing assay, none of the subjects developed neutralizing
antibodies. Potential impact of antitarlatamab antibodies on the PK, clinical efficacy, and
clinical safety of tarlatamab was assessed with the following results:
•

Based on the population PK analysis, individuals with positive antitarlatamab
antibody binding status at any time during the study were estimated to have
approximately 21% higher clearance, which translated to an approximately 24%
decrease in serum trough concentrations on average. However, this difference in
exposures did not have a clinically relevant impact on efficacy.

•

At the proposed dosing regimen, the efficacy measures (including ORR, DCR, and
DOR) were comparable for antitarlatamab binding antibody positive subjects
compared with those who were antitarlatamab antibody negative.

•

There was no apparent impact of antitarlatamab antibody development on safety
based on assessment of adverse events.

6.3.2.

Clinical Pharmacology Questions

6.3.2.1 Does the clinical pharmacology program provide supportive evidence
of effectiveness?

Data:
Data from Study 20200491 (phase 2) and Study 20160323 (FIH) were included in an
integrated exposure-response analysis to support the selection of the proposed dosing
regimen (summarized in Section 3.4 of the Clinical Overview and detailed in Section 3.5
of Module 2.7.2, Summary of Clinical Pharmacology). Relationships between tarlatamab
exposure and selected measures of response (ORR, DCR, BTSR, PFS, OS, and DOR) were
evaluated. The tarlatamab exposure metrics evaluated as predictors of response were
model-predicted average concentrations over the first cycle (Cavg), peak serum
concentrations over the first cycle (Cmax), and trough serum concentrations at the end of
the first cycle on day 28 (Ctrough), generated using a population PK model. Covariates
included in the analyses were demographic factors (age, body weight, sex, race,
ethnicity), organ impairment (normal, mild, moderate renal and hepatic function),
baseline serum albumin, disease status (ECOG and sum of tumor lesion diameters at
baseline), number of prior therapies, and positive ADA status.
With regards to evidence of effectiveness, integrated analysis using pooled data from
Study 20160323 and Study 20200491 show that:
•

Significant exposure-response relationships were established for key efficacy
measures including ORR, DCR, BTSR, PFS, and OS.

•

Near maximal efficacy was observed at exposures associated with the proposed
clinical regimen of 10 mg Q2W.

•

Exposures associated with lower doses are estimated to have lower efficacy.

The Applicant’s Position:
Overall, results of exposure-response analysis for efficacy measures suggest the
proposed dosing regimen of 10 mg Q2W exhibited near maximal efficacy in the
intended patient population, and exposures associated with lower doses are estimated
to have lower efficacy. None of the covariates evaluated in this analysis resulted in
clinically meaningful changes in efficacy and therefore do not warrant a dose
adjustment.
The FDA’s Assessment:
FDA agrees with the Applicant’s position on that E-R analyses, in addition to the clinical
findings from Studies 20200491 and 20160323, supported the proposed 1/10 mg dosing
regimen. In Study 20160323 dose escalation, 0.003 to 100 mg Q2W were explored.

8 Statistical and Clinical Evaluation
8.1. Review of Relevant Individual Trials Used to Support Efficacy
8.1.1.

Pivotal Study 20200491

Trial Design
The Applicant’s Description:
Study 20200491 is an ongoing phase 2, open-label, registrational study evaluating tarlatamab
for the treatment of subjects with recurrent SCLC who had progressed or recurred following 1
platinum-based regimen (with or without immune checkpoint inhibitor) and at least 1 other line
of therapy (retreatment with a platinum-based regimen was considered a second line of
therapy) (Figure 13).
This study was conducted in 3 parts. Part 1 evaluated 2 dose levels of tarlatamab in which
approximately 180 eligible subjects were planned to be enrolled and randomized 1:1 to either
treatment with a target dose of 10 or 100 mg tarlatamab. To select a dose for expansion, an
interim analysis was performed after 30 subjects in each group in Part 1 had opportunity to
confirm an objective response after the first posttreatment scan, or up to 13 weeks of followup, whichever occurred first. Enrollment continued in a randomized fashion until dose selection
was finalized. Part 2 was a dose expansion phase at the selected dose based on the interim
Version date: June 2022 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)
Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are
completed by the Applicant and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.
Reference ID: 5382024

121

NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {BLA 761344}
{Tarlatamab}
analysis of Part 1 and enrolled until approximately 100 subjects (from Parts 1 and 2 combined)
had been enrolled at the selected dose level. Part 3 was initiated after completing enrollment
of Parts 1 and 2, to enroll up to approximately 30 additional subjects at the selected dose, with
modified cycle 1 monitoring criteria.
Figure 13. Applicant – Schema of Study 20200491

*Once 30 subjects per dose level had the opportunity to confirm an objective response after the first posttreatment scan or ³ 13 weeks of follow-up, whichever occurred first.
BICR = blinded independent central review; DCR = disease control rate; DOR = duration of response;
ECOG PS = Eastern Cooperative Oncology Group performance status; ES-SCLC = extensive-stage small cell lung
cancer; ITT = intention-to-treat; ORR = objective response rate; OS = overall survival;
PFS = progression-free survival; Q2W = every 2 weeks; R = randomization; RECIST = Response Evaluation Citeria in
Solid Tumors; TEAE = treatment-emergent adverse event

The FDA’s Assessment:
FDA generally agrees with the Applicant’s description of the trial design of Study 20200491.
Eligibility Criteria
The Applicant’s Description:
Study 20200491
Eligible subjects were men or women (≥ 18 years of age [or legal adult age within country]) with
histologically or cytologically confirmed relapsed or refractory SCLC who progressed or recurred
following 1 platinum-based regimen and at least 1 other prior line of therapy (Note: [1]
re-treatment with a platinum-based regimen was considered a second line of therapy; [2]
platinum-based regimen followed by immune checkpoint/PD-L1 inhibitor as maintenance

Reference ID: 5382024

NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {BLA 761344}
{Tarlatamab}
therapy was considered 1 line of therapy; [3] in countries where standard of care first-line
systemic treatment included platinum-containing chemotherapy in combination with PD-L1
inhibitor, it was required for subjects to have failed or be ineligible for PD-L1 inhibitor as part of
their first-line systemic treatment).
The FDA’s Assessment:
FDA generally agrees with the Applicant’s description of key eligibility criteria for Study
20200491. Additional key inclusion criteria include patients having an ECOG performance status
of 0 or 1 and at least one measurable lesion as defined by RECIST v1.1. Key exclusion criteria
included symptomatic brain metastases, evidence of interstitial lung disease or non-infectious
pneumonitis and active immunodeficiency.
Study Endpoints
The Applicant’s Description:
Study 20200491
The primary objectives of the study were to:
• evaluate safety and efficacy (per RECIST 1.1 by investigator) of 2 dose levels of tarlatamab
(Part 1 only)
• evaluate antitumor activity of tarlatamab as determined by ORR per RECIST 1.1 by blinded
independent central review (BICR) (Parts 1 and 2)
• evaluate safety of reduced mandatory monitoring period in cycle 1 at selected dose of
tarlatamab (Part 3)
Secondary objectives for all parts of the study included other measures of efficacy (endpoints of
DOR, DCR, PFS, and OS), safety, PK, and immunogenicity.
The FDA’s Assessment:
FDA agrees with the Applicant’s description of the endpoints in Study 20200491. FDA considers
ORR an appropriate efficacy endpoint to support accelerated approval, where ORR is of
sufficient magnitude and duration with a corresponding 95% confidence interval excluding
clinically irrelevant response rates for the proposed dose (10 mg dose). Additionally, while the
statistical analysis plan included summary statistics of PFS and OS as secondary efficacy
endpoints, FDA does not consider time to-event endpoints interpretable in the context of a
single arm trial for the purposes of regulatory decision-making.

The FDA’s Assessment:
FDA agrees with the Applicant’s descriptions of the statistical analysis plan. In addition, during
the pre-BLA meeting dated September 8, 2023, FDA requested that the Applicant provide a 95%
CI of ORR using the Clopper-Pearson exact method, and FDA also commented that the astreated population, which includes all patients who received at least one dose of tarlatamab,
should be the primary efficacy analysis population. In the BLA submission, the Applicant
provided 95% CI and included results of a post-hoc analysis of ORR and DOR in the as-treated
population. FDA and the Applicant also agreed that it would be acceptable to provide updated
efficacy data in the 90-day safety update report, which were provided during the review cycle.

Study Results – Study 20200491

Compliance with Good Clinical Practices
Data:
Audits of Study 20200491 were included as part of the independent Amgen Quality,
Compliance, and Audit program performed by Amgen. The audit certificate is provided in
Section 16.1.8 of Study 20200491 clinical study reports (CSRs).
Amgen has been closely monitoring the evolving COVID-19 situation across the globe and has,
therefore, taken proactive steps to maintain the safety of study participants and support staff,
including Amgen representatives, at all clinical study centers, and to maintain study data
integrity while maintaining compliance with good clinical practice (GCP). Guidelines on study
conduct during the COVID-19 period were documented in the respective study protocols.
The Applicant’s Position:
Study 20200491 was conducted in accordance with the Code of Federal Regulations (CFR)
governing the protection of human subjects (21 CFR part 50), Institutional Review Boards
(21 CFR part 56), and the obligations of clinical investigators (21 CFR 312.50 to 312.70) in
accordance with GCP. Study 20200491 was conducted under Investigational New Drug 134859.
The FDA’s Assessment:
FDA generally agrees with the Applicant’s position. Findings were noted from the GCP site
inspections as described in Section 4.1. The Applicant conducted a 100% SDR/SDV for all clinical
sites that participated in Study 20200491 which yielded updated data for efficacy and safety
that did not impact FDA’s interpretation of the trial results in a meaningful way. This updated
data was used in labeling. Based on the information provided in this application, the conduct of
Study 20200491 was consistent with the International Council for Harmonization guidelines for
Good Clinical Practice, U.S. Federal regulations and requirements by other applicable regulatory
authorities.
Financial Disclosure
Data:
See Appendix 19.2.
The Applicant’s Position:
Study 20200491 was an Amgen-sponsored clinical study. Financial interests or arrangements
with clinical investigators have been disclosed (see Appendix 19.2).
The FDA’s Assessment:

Reference ID: 5382024

NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {BLA 761344}
{Tarlatamab}
FDA reviewed the financial disclosure information provided by the Applicant. Refer to FDA’s
assessment of this information in Appendix 19.2.
Patient Disposition
Study 20200491: Overall, as of the data cutoff date of 27 June 2023, 222 subjects were
randomized (Part 1) or enrolled (Parts 2 and 3) and 220 subjects received at least 1 dose of
tarlatamab, including 99 subjects in the 10 mg target dose group across Parts 1 and 2,
87 subjects in the 100 mg target dose group in Part 1, and 34 subjects in the Part 3 modified
safety monitoring 10 mg target dose group.
As of the data cutoff date, 32.0% of subjects overall were continuing treatment with
tarlatamab: 29.0% from the 10 mg group (Parts 1 and 2), 25.0% from the 100 mg group (Part
1), and 58.8% from Part 3 (10 mg with modified monitoring). Overall, 67.1% of subjects had
discontinued tarlatamab: 70.0% from the 10 mg group (Parts 1 and 2), 73.9% from the 100 mg
group (Part 1), and 41.2% from Part 3 (10 mg with modified monitoring). The most common
reason for discontinuation of tarlatamab was disease progression (46.8%), followed by adverse
event (6.8%), death (5.9%), and subject request (5.9%). As of the data cutoff date, 43.2% of
subjects had discontinued the study; the most common reasons for study discontinuation were
death (32.9%) and withdrawal of consent (9.0%) (Table 14-1.1.1 of Study 20200491).
The Applicant’s Position:
As of the data cutoff dates for the Biologics License Application (BLA), 220 subjects were
enrolled and received tarlatamab in Study 20200491 (99 at the 10 mg dose from Parts 1 and 2;
34 at the 10 mg dose with modified safety monitoring from Part 3). The primary analysis for the
pivotal study was based on the BICR Full Analysis Set which included subjects who were
randomized (Part 1) or enrolled (Part 2), received at least 1 dose of tarlatamab, and had 1 or
more measurable lesions at baseline as assessed by BICR using RECIST 1.1. Safety analyses
included subjects who received ³ 1 dose of investigational product.
The FDA’s Assessment:
FDA generally agrees with the Applicant’s description of patient disposition for Study 20200491.
As previously discussed, although the Applicant presented data from the 10 and 100 mg doses
for tarlatamab throughout this application, FDA review of efficacy focused on the proposed
indicated dose (10 mg) for tarlatamab.
As of the data cutoff date, the most common reason for discontinuation of tarlatamab 10 mg
was disease progression (52%), followed by adverse event (7%), death (3%), and subject
request (6%). In addition, 43% of subjects had discontinued the study; the most common
reasons for study discontinuation were death (35%) and withdrawal of consent (7%)
An information request was also sent to the Applicant to clarify the details for 8% of patients
who discontinued safety follow-up with the reason “other”. The Applicant clarified that the
majority of patients refused the safety follow-up visit.

unlikely to have impacted the study’s results in a meaningful way.

Data:
Study 20200491: Baseline demographics and disease characteristics were generally consistent

were men, and the majority were White (62.7%) and not Hispanic or Latino (61.4%). The
median (range) age was 64 (34 to 82) years, and the majority of subjects were younger than
65 years (52.7%).

The Applicant’s Position:
Overall, the subjects with SCLC enrolled in Study 20200491 had baseline characteristics
indicative of subjects with advanced SCLC. Thus, the patient population evaluated in the
efficacy studies is considered representative of the overall population of patients with
advanced SCLC and supportive of the proposed indication.
Data from racial and ethnic minorities such as American Indian or Alaska Native, Black or
African American, and Native Hawaiian or Other Pacific Islander are limited.
The FDA’s Assessment:
The median age of patients enrolled is consistent with that of the US patient population with
SCLC. A higher proportion of male patients were enrolled compared with female patients (71%
vs. 28%) which does not align with the known distribution of sex for SCLC in the US. However,
the ORR was similar between both groups of patients (38% for males vs. 46% for females).
Patient enrollment into the efficacy population was multi-regional and global; the countries
with the highest enrollment/randomization included: South Korea (30%), Spain (18%) and Japan
(11%). Of the overall study population, no Black/African American patients were enrolled and
only one patient was Hispanic/Latino. The distribution underrepresents the incidence of SCLC in
these racial and ethnic minority groups. Despite these limitations, FDA agrees that the
submitted data is supportive of the proposed indication for the US population. However, a
postmarketing commitment (PMC) will be issued for analyses from ongoing or planned clinical
trials enrolling a sufficient representation of U.S. racial and ethnic minority patients that is
reflective of the US population of patients with SCLC to further characterize the safety and
efficacy of tarlatamab in these patients.

NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {BLA 761344}
{Tarlatamab}
Other Baseline Characteristics (e.g., disease characteristics, important concomitant drugs)
Data:
20200491
A total of 220 subjects received tarlatamab in this study with 99 subjects receiving 10 mg IV
Q2W across Part 1 and Part 2 (combined), and treatment continued until disease progression or
unacceptable toxicity. Tumor assessments were performed every 6 weeks for the first 48 weeks
and every 12 weeks thereafter. Overall across all parts of the study, subjects had a median
(range) of 2 (1 to 8) prior lines of therapy, including prior PD-1 or PD-L1 inhibitor therapy
(74.1%) and prior radiotherapy (79.1%) (Table 30). Time to progression after first-line platinum
therapy was < 90 days for 51 subjects (23.2%), 90 to < 180 days for 47 subjects
(21.4%), 180 days or more for 47 subjects (21.4%), and unknown for 75 subjects (34.1%). Most
subjects had metastatic disease (95.5%), with no brain metastases (73.6%) or liver metastases
(64.1%), and an ECOG score of 1 (72.7%). In the 10 mg group, 99 subjects (100%) received prior
platinum therapy, 20.2% received prior topotecan therapy, and 69.7% received prior anti-PD-L1
therapy. Baseline characteristics were generally consistent across the dose groups.
A post hoc platinum sensitivity analysis was performed for Parts 1 and 2 for progression-free
interval after first-line platinum therapy, in which the interval was defined as time from end of
platinum therapy in the first line to date of progression or start date of subsequent therapy,
whichever came first. Of 185 subjects, progression-free interval was < 90 days for 60 subjects
(38 subjects at 10 mg dose), ≥ 90 days for 109 subjects (55 subjects at 10 mg dose), and
unknown for 16 subjects (6 subjects at 10 mg dose) (Table 22 of Module 2.7.3, SCE ).
A post hoc sensitivity analysis was also performed for Parts 1 and 2 for prior lines of therapy
representing global standard of care. Of 185 subjects, the number of subjects who received the
following therapies at any line were 41 for topotecan, 96 for any topoisomerase 1 inhibitor
(topotecan or belotecan or irinotecan), 13 for lurbinectedin, and 17 for amrubicin (Table 23 of
Module 2.7.3, SCE).

radical treatment

Treatment Compliance, Concomitant Medications, and Rescue Medication Use
Data:
Treatment Compliance
Study 20200491: When subjects were dosed at the site, they received tarlatamab directly
from the investigator or designee, under medical supervision. The date and time of each
dose administered were recorded in the source documents and in the electronic case report
form (eCRF). Additional details on the method used to assess treatment compliance are
provided in Protocol Section 6.5.
Concomitant Medications
Throughout the study, investigators could prescribe any concomitant medications or
treatments deemed necessary to provide adequate supportive care except for those listed
in Protocol Section 6.1.6 of Study 20200491.
Study 20200491: Overall, concomitant medication use was reported for 217 subjects
(98.6%). The concomitant medications (used in ≥ 10% of subjects) are presented in Table
12-15 of Study 20200491. Of these, the most common (≥ 20% of subjects) were
paracetamol (82.3%), dexamethasone (42.3%), sodium chloride (29.1%), magnesium oxide
(25.5%), omeprazole (22.7%), lactulose (22.3%), and metoclopramide (20.0%). Concomitant
CRS medication use by preferred term is provided in Table 14-8.11 of Study 20200491.
Rescue Medication
Not applicable
The Applicant’s Position:
No formal treatment compliance measurements were planned. Analyses for exposure
demonstrated that, the median relative dose intensity of tarlatamab was 93% and 90% for
the 10 and 100 mg dose groups, respectively, in Study 20200491. Concomitant medication
use was consistent with protocol-specified criteria.
The FDA’s Assessment:
FDA agrees with the Applicant’s description of treatment compliance along with concomitant
and rescue medications. In addition, pretreatment with dexamethasone 1 hour prior to
administration of tarlatamab on Cycle 1 Day 1 and Day 8 and treatment with 1 liter of normal
saline over 4 to 5 hours following all tarlatamab doses in Cycle 1 was required for all patients
per protocol.

{Tarlatamab}
Efficacy Results – Primary Endpoint (Including Sensitivity Analyses)
Data:
Study 20200491
A total of 185 and 186 subjects were included in the BICR or Investigator Full
Analysis/Safety Analysis Set, respectively. The key efficacy results for tumor response
from the phase 2 Study 20200491 (safety analysis set) Part 1 and 2 are provided in
Table 32.
Objective response was defined as a best overall response of either complete response
or partial response per RECIST 1.1. Of the 186 evaluable subjects in the Safety Analysis
Set for Parts 1 and 2 (10 and 100 mg), 7 subjects (3.8%) had a confirmed complete
response and 63 subjects (33.9%) had a confirmed partial response. The ORR (95% CI)
by BICR was 41.4% (31.6, 51.8) for subjects who received the 10 mg target dose and
33.3% (23.6, 44.3) for subjects who received the 100 mg target dose. Within the 10 mg
dose, the lower limit of the 95% CI excluded the prespecified benchmark ORR of 15%.
The overall ORR (95% CI) by BICR was 37.6% (30.7, 45.0) (Table 32).
Duration of response was defined as time from first evidence of complete response or
partial response to disease progression or death due to any cause, whichever occurred
first. For Parts 1 and 2 overall (10 and 100 mg), the median (95% CI) DOR among
confirmed responders as assessed by BICR was 8.3 (6.9, not estimable [NE]) months, and
the median (95% CI) follow-up time for DOR was 9.5 (8.2, 9.7) months (Table 8 of
Module 2.7.3, SCE). The median (range) time to objective response was
1.4 (1.1 to 9.6) months. In the 10 mg group, the median (95% CI) DOR was not reached
(5.9, NE months) (range: 2.4-12.4+ months). In the 100 mg group, it was 7.6 (5.6, NE
months) (range: 1.4+ to 12.6+). The median (95% CI) follow-up time for DOR was 8.3
(7.2, 9.6) months in the 10 mg group and 9.7 (8.2, 9.9) months in the 100 mg group. The
overall median time to objective response was 1.4 months in both dose groups (Table 8
of Module 2.7.3, SCE).
Of the 41 responders in the 10 mg target dose group, 23 subjects (56.1%) and
10 subjects (24.4%) had a DOR of at least 6 and 9 months, respectively. As of
27 June 2023, 22 of 41 responders (53.7%) in the 10 mg target dose group were still
having ongoing responses (on treatment without disease progression or death),
including 18 subjects (43.9%) whose responses reached at least 6 months and still had
ongoing response (Table 14-4.3.2 of Study 20200491).
Of the 33 subjects in the BICR Interim RECIST Analysis Set for Part 3, 4 subjects (12.1%)
had a confirmed partial response. As of the data cutoff date, 5 subjects (15.2%) had an
unconfirmed partial response awaiting a confirmatory scan. The overall ORR by BICR
using a 97.5% CI was 12.1% (2.8%, 30.6%) for confirmed responders and
141

confirmatory scan (Table 14-4.7.2 of Study 20200491).

Efficacy Update as of 02 October 2023
As of the data cutoff date 02 October 2023, of the 186 subjects in the Safety Analysis Set for
Parts 1 and 2, 8 subjects (4.3%) had a confirmed complete response and 62 subjects (33.3%)
had a confirmed partial response as assessed by BICR (Table 33.). The overall ORR (95% CI) by
BICR was 37.6% (30.7%, 45.0%). The ORR (95% CI) was 41.4% (31.6%, 51.8%) for subjects who
received the 10 mg target dose and 33.3% (23.6%, 44.3%) for subjects who received the 100 mg
target dose (Table 33. and Figure 16). These results are consistent with those from the primary
analysis (Table 32).
In Parts 1 and 2 as assessed by investigator, no subject had a confirmed complete response and
70 subjects (37.6%) had a confirmed partial response. The ORR (95% CI) was 41.4% (31.6%,
51.8%) for subjects who received the 10 mg target dose and 33.3% (23.6%, 44.3%) for subjects
who received the 100 mg target dose. The overall ORR (95% CI) by investigator assessment was
37.6% (30.7%, 45.0%) (90-day Table 14-4.1.1 of Study 20200491 and 90-day Figure 14-4.1.1 of
Study 20200491).
Analyses were conducted for best overall response to evaluate concordance between
assessments by BICR (primary endpoint) and the study center investigator (secondary
endpoint). The concordance rate between BICR and investigator assessment for responder
(complete response or partial response) vs nonresponder was 89.0% (90-day Table 14-4.2 of
Study 20200491).
For Parts 1 and 2 overall, the median (95% CI) DOR among confirmed responders as assessed by
BICR was 9.7 (6.9, NE) months (8.3 [6.9, NE] months at primary analysis). In the 10 mg target
dose group (Parts 1 and 2), the response appears rapid and durable with median (range) time
to response (TTR) of 1.4 (1.1 to 2.8) months and a median (95% CI) DOR of 9.7 (5.9, NE) months.
Of the 41 responders in the 10 mg target dose group, 27 subjects (65.9%) and 17 subjects
(41.5%) had a DOR of at least 6 and 9 months, respectively; 11 subjects (26.8%) had a DOR of at
least 12 months, all increased since primary analysis. As of 02 October 2023,
19 of 41 responders (46.3%) in the 10 mg target dose group were still having ongoing responses
(on treatment without disease progression or death), including 15 subjects (36.6%) whose
responses reached at least 9 months and were still ongoing (an increase from 10 subjects at
primary analysis) (90-day Table 14-4.3.2 of Study 20200491).

Efficacy Results – Secondary and other relevant endpoints
Data:
Study 20200491
The PFS and OS results provide additional evidence of the efficacy of tarlatamab. In
Study 20200491 Parts 1 and 2 overall, the median (95% CI) PFS by BICR was
4.0 (2.9, 5.4) months, with a median follow-up time of 9.7 months, and Kaplan-Meier
estimates for PFS of 36.1% at 6 months and 23.4% at 12 months (Table 14-4.5.2 of Study
20200491 and Section 2.1.4.4 of Module 2.7.3, SCE). Results for the 10 and 100 mg dose
groups (Parts 1 and 2) were comparable: in the 10 mg dose group, the median (95% CI)
PFS by BICR was 4.3 (3.0, 5.6) months, with a median follow-up time of 9.7 months, and
Kaplan-Meier estimates for PFS of 39.7% at 6 months and 25.7% at 12 months; in the
100 mg dose group, the median (95% CI) PFS by BICR was 3.2 (2.6, 4.2) months, with a
median follow-up time of 10.8 months, and Kaplan-Meier estimates for PFS of 31.9% at
6 months and 20.7% at 12 months.
Median (range) follow-up time for OS for Parts 1 and 2 overall was 10.6 (9.7 to 11.3)
months, and the median (95% CI) OS was not reached (12.4 to NE months); the
Kaplan-Meier estimate for OS at 6 months was 72.5% and 59.8% at 12 months, with
similar results for the 10 and 100 mg dose groups (Table 14-4.6.1 of Study 20200491 and
Section 2.1.4.5 of Module 2.7.3, SCE). As of the data cutoff date, 101 of the 186 subjects
(54.3%) were alive at last follow-up visit, 19 (10.2%) had withdrawn consent, 1 (0.5%)
was lost to follow-up, and 65 (34.9%) had died (Table 14-4.6.1 of Study 20200491).

Reference ID: 5382024

NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {BLA 761344}
{Tarlatamab}
Efficacy Update as of 02 October 2023
In Study 20200491 Parts 1 and 2 overall, the Kaplan-Meier estimate of median (95% CI) PFS by
BICR was 4.0 (2.9, 5.4) months (90-day Table 14-4.5.2 of Study 20200491). In the 10 mg dose
group (Parts 1 and 2), median (95% CI) PFS based on BICR was 4.3 (3.0, 5.6) months, with a
median follow-up time of 13.6 months (90-day Table 14-4.5.2 of Study 20200491 and
Figure 19). The Kaplan-Meier estimates for PFS at 6 and 12 months were 39.7% and 25.2%,
respectively. In the 100 mg target dose group, median (95% CI) PFS based on BICR was
3.2 (2.6, 4.6) months, with a median follow-up time of 11.8 months (90-day Table 14-4.5.2 of
Study 20200491 and Figure 19). The Kaplan-Meier estimates for PFS at 6 and 12 months were
32.8% and 19.7%, respectively. Except for a longer median follow-up time, these results are
similar to those from the primary analysis (Table 14-4.6.1 of Study 20200491).
For Parts 1 and 2 overall, median (range) follow-up time for OS was 13.6 (12.9, 14.3) months,
and the median (95% CI) OS was 15.2 (12.3, NE) months (previously not reached at primary
analysis). In the 10 mg dose group (Parts 1 and 2), median (95% CI) OS was 15.2 (10.8, NE)
months, with a median follow-up time of 13.8 months (90-day Table 14-4.6.1 of Study
20200491 and Figure 20). In comparison, at the primary analysis, the median OS was lower
(14.3 months) and the median follow-up time shorter (10.6 months). The Kaplan-Meier
estimates for OS at 6 and 12 months were 73.4% and 56.7%, respectively. In the 100 mg dose
group, median (95% CI) OS was 15.1 (10.1, NE) months (previously not reached), with a median
follow-up time of 13.3 months (previously 10.6 months) (90-day Table 14-4.6.1 of Study
20200491 and Figure 20). The Kaplan-Meier estimates for OS at 6 and 12 months were 71.4%
and 61.8%, respectively.
As of data cutoff date 02 October 2023, 48 subjects (48.5%) and 38 subjects (43.7%) in the
10 and 100 mg groups, respectively, were alive at last follow-up (90-day Table 14-4.6.1 of Study
20200491). This number is decreased from the number of subjects alive at the primary analysis
(57 and 44 in the 10 and 100 mg groups, respectively). Except for a longer median follow-up
time and fewer subjects alive at last follow-up, these results are similar to those from the
primary analysis.

Reference ID: 5382024

NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {BLA 761344}
{Tarlatamab}
The Applicant’s Position:
The PFS and OS results in the pivotal study provides additional evidence of the efficacy of
tarlatamab. No notable differences were observed among various subgroups for ORR, DOR,
DoDc, PFS, or OS. However, subgroup analysis interpretation may be limited because of the
small sample size of each subgroup.
The FDA’s Assessment:
DoR was also a secondary endpoint for the study; FDA’s assessment of the DoR endpoint is
summarized above along with assessment of data from the ORR endpoint. FDA reviewed the
summary of the PFS and OS data above. However, FDA disagrees with the Applicant’s position
regarding the interpretation of PFS and OS results as evidence for treatment effect. These
endpoints are difficult to interpret in the absence of an appropriate control arm.
The interpretation of the comparison of the PFS and OS results between the 10 mg dose group
and 100 mg dose group in the BICR Full Analysis Set and Safety Analysis Set are also limited, as
the two groups of patients were not fully randomized (the 10 mg group included patients in
Part 2 who enrolled after dose selection and thus were not randomized).
Dose/Dose Response
See Section 6.3.2, Clinical Pharmacology Questions
The Applicant’s Position:
The proposed dose of 10 mg Q2W is effective and generally well tolerated in subjects with
advanced SCLC. The proposed dosing regimen is supported by the observed efficacy and safety
data and demonstrates a favorable benefit-risk profile. Overall, results of exposure-response
analysis for efficacy measures suggest the proposed dosing regimen of 10 mg Q2W exhibited
near maximal efficacy in the intended patient population, and exposures associated with lower
doses are estimated to have lower efficacy. None of the covariates evaluated in the exposureresponse analysis resulted in clinically meaningful changes in efficacy and therefore do not
warrant a dose adjustment.
The FDA’s Assessment:
FDA generally agrees with the Applicant’s position that E-R analyses, in addition to the clinical
findings from Studies 20200491 and 20160323, support the proposed 1/10 mg dosing regimen.
Please see Section 6.2.2.1 for FDA’s full assessment on the proposed tarlatamab dose.

Efficacy Results – Secondary or exploratory COA (PRO) endpoints
Data:
An exploratory objective of the phase 2 Study 20200491 was to investigate the subject
experience with tarlatamab treatment using patient-reported outcome instruments. Although
exploratory, a trend toward improvement or stabilization was observed in the severity of key
lung cancer symptoms of cough, dyspnea, and chest pain across both doses (10 and 100 mg in
Part 1 and Part 2) when compared with baseline (Section 10.4 and Section 16.1.13.8 of
Study 20200491). A consistent trend toward improvement was also observed for global health
status. Most subjects reported no or little degree of bother due to treatment-related side
effects associated with tarlatamab.
The Applicant’s Position:
Although exploratory, subjects overall showed a trend toward improvement or stabilization was
observed in the severity of key lung cancer symptoms of cough, dyspnea, and chest pain when
compared with baseline.

8.1.3.

Supportive Study 20160323

Trial Design
The Applicant’s Description:
Study 20160323 is an ongoing, phase 1, FIH, open-label, ascending, multiple-dose study
evaluating tarlatamab as monotherapy and in combination with anti-PD-1 therapy
(Figure 21). Tarlatamab is administered as an IV infusion Q2W or Q3W (in Part G) (with
or without step dosing) in subjects with advanced SCLC that progressed or recurred
after at least 1 platinum-based regimen. The study consists of multiple parts designed to
evaluate the safety and tolerability of tarlatamab alone (Parts A, D, E, and G) or in
combination with pembrolizumab (Part C) and to determine the maximum tolerated
dose or recommended phase 2 dose of tarlatamab alone (Part A1) or in combination
with pembrolizumab (Part C). Secondary objectives were to characterize the PK and
evaluate preliminary antitumor activity of tarlatamab alone or in combination with
pembrolizumab (Figure 21). See Section 8.1 of Study 20160323 for full details of the
study design.

Reference ID: 5382024

NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {BLA 761344}
{Tarlatamab}
The FDA’s Assessment:
FDA agrees with the Applicant’s description of the study design.
Eligibility Criteria
The Applicant’s Description:
Study 20160323
Eligible subjects were ³18 years of age with relapsed or refractory SCLC who progressed or
recurred following at least 1 platinum-based regimen. Subjects must have had measurable
disease per modified Response Evaluation Criteria in Solid Tumors (RECIST) version 1.1, an
ECOG performance status of 0 to 2, and adequate organ function. Eligibility criteria included
adequate cardiac, pulmonary, kidney, and liver function. Subjects with acute and/or
uncontrolled active systemic infection, active hepatitis B or C, or human immunodeficiency
virus infection or COVID-19 infection were excluded.
The FDA’s Assessment:
FDA agrees with the Applicant’s description of the eligibility criteria.
Study Endpoints
The Applicant’s Description:
Study 20160323: The primary objectives of the study were to evaluate the safety and
tolerability of tarlatamab and to determine the maximum tolerated dose or recommended
phase 2 dose of tarlatamab. Secondary objectives included characterizing the PK of tarlatamab
and evaluating preliminary antitumor activity of tarlatamab.
The FDA’s Assessment:
FDA agrees with the Applicant’s description of the study endpoints.
Statistical Analysis Plan and Amendments
The Applicant’s Description:
Study 20160323: There were 3 amendments to the original SAP before the data cutoff date of
28 March 2023 for this study as summarized in Table 8-8 of Study 20160323. Full statistical
methods for this supportive study are provided in Section 8.8 of Study 20160323.
The FDA’s Assessment:
FDA agrees with the Applicant’s above descriptions of the statistical analysis plan and
amendments.

Study Results – Study 20160323

Compliance with Good Clinical Practices
Data:
Audits of Study 20160323 were included as part of the independent Amgen Quality,
Compliance, and Audit program performed by Amgen. The audit certificates are provided in
Section 16.1.8 of Study 20160323 clinical study reports.
Amgen has been closely monitoring the evolving COVID-19 situation across the globe and has,
therefore, taken proactive steps to maintain the safety of study participants and support staff,
including Amgen representatives, at all clinical study centers, and to maintain study data
integrity while maintaining compliance with GCP. Guidelines on study conduct during the
COVID-19 period were documented in the respective study protocols.
The Applicant’s Position:
Study 20160323 was conducted in accordance with the CFR governing the protection of human
subjects (21 CFR part 50), Institutional Review Boards (21 CFR part 56), and the obligations of
clinical investigators (21 CFR 312.50 to 312.70) in accordance with GCP. Study 20160323 was
conducted under Investigational New Drug 134859.
The FDA’s Assessment:
FDA agrees with the Applicant’s position.

The Applicant’s Position:
Study 20160323 was an Amgen-sponsored clinical study. Financial interests or arrangements
with clinical investigators have been disclosed (see Appendix 19.2).
The FDA’s Assessment:
FDA agrees with the Applicant’s position. See Appendix 19.2 for full review.
Patient Disposition
Study 20160323: Overall, as of the data cutoff date of 28 March 2023, 208 subjects were
enrolled in Study 20160323, and 205 subjects received at least 1 dose of tarlatamab, including
27 subjects in the 10 mg Q2W dose group, 63 subjects in the 100 mg Q2W dose group, and
13 subjects in the 100 mg Q2W dose group with modified safety monitoring. As of the data

Reference ID: 5382024

NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {BLA 761344}
{Tarlatamab}
cutoff date, 180 subjects (86.5%) discontinued tarlatamab. The most frequent reason for
discontinuation was disease progression (68.8%) (Table 14-1.1 of Study 20160323).
The Applicant’s Position:
As of the data cutoff dates for the Biologics License Application (BLA), 205 subjects were
enrolled and received tarlatamab in Study 20160323 (27 at the 10 mg dose).
The FDA’s Assessment:
FDA agrees with the Applicant’s description of patient disposition. The focus of FDA’s review for
this study will be based upon the patients who received tarlatamab at the proposed indicated
dose (10 mg dose).
Protocol Violations/Deviations
Data:
Study 20160323: As of the data cutoff date 28 March 2023, a total of 158 subjects (77.1%) had
at least 1 IPD. Important protocol deviations were consistent across groups, with the exception
of subjects in the 10 mg monotherapy dose group who had a more than 10% higher rate of IPDs
(88.9%) than the overall population and subjects in the 100 mg dose with 24-hour monitoring
group who had a more than 10% lower rate of IPDs (53.8%) than the overall population (Section
9.2 of Study 20160323). Overall, the most frequently reported (> 10% of subjects) IPDs were
missing data (65.9%) and received the wrong treatment or incorrect dose (10.2%).
The Applicant’s Position:
The above mentioned IPDs did not affect the overall results of the study.
The FDA’s Assessment:
FDA agrees with the Applicant’s position. From the 27 patients who received the tarlatamab 10
mg dose, 24 patients (89%) reported at least one IPD. The most frequently reported (>10% of
patients) IPDs were missing data 70.4% and receiving the wrong treatment or incorrect dose
(11.4%) in the 10 mg dose group.
Table of Demographic Characteristics
Data:
Study 20160323: In the overall population (N= 205), more than half of subjects (56.1%)
were men, mostly White (78.0%), and not Hispanic or Latino (77.6%). The median
(range) age was 63 (32 to 80) years, with more than half of all subjects younger than
65 years (60.5%) (Table 35).

Other Baseline Characteristics (e.g., disease characteristics, important concomitant drugs)
Data:
Study 20160323
Overall, subjects had a median (range) of 2.0 (1 to 7) prior lines of therapy, including prior PD-1
or PD-L1 inhibitor therapy (61.0%). Most subjects had received prior radiotherapy for current
malignancy (77.1%). Most subjects had metastatic disease (92.2%) and an ECOG performance
score of 1 (61.5%). Prior to tarlatamab treatment, subjects’ response to their most recent
platinum-based anticancer treatment was as follows:
•

47.3% of subjects were sensitive to the most recent platinum-based therapy, defined as
best response of stable disease or better and an interval of ³ 90 days between the date of
the last dose of platinum in the most recent platinum-containing prior line and the start
date.

•

28.3% of subjects were refractory, defined as a best response of progressive disease to
most recent line of platinum-based chemotherapy.

•

20.0% of subjects were resistant, defined as a best response of stable disease or better and
a treatment-free interval of < 90 days between the date of most recent line of
platinum-based chemotherapy and the start date of the next line of treatment.

Baseline characteristics for Study 20160323 are provided in Table 13 of Module 2.7.3, SCE.
The Applicant’s Position:
Overall, the subjects with SCLC enrolled across phase 1 and 2 studies had baseline
characteristics indicative of subjects with advanced SCLC. Thus, the patient population
evaluated in the efficacy studies is considered representative of the overall population of
patients with advanced SCLC and supportive of the proposed indication.
The FDA’s Assessment:
FDA reviewed the results presented above for the other baseline characteristics but did not
verify the results since this study was supportive and the results were not included in labeling.

Throughout the study, investigators could prescribe any concomitant medications or

Overall, concomitant medication use was reported for 204 of 205 subjects (99.5%). The
concomitant medication categories with the highest incidence rates (> 10% of subjects) are
presented in Table 12-14 of Study 20160323. Overall, the most frequently reported
concomitant medications (≥ 25% of subjects) were paracetamol (85.9%), dexamethasone
(82.4%), sodium chloride (56.6%), ondansetron (32.2%), and omeprazole (28.3%).

Not applicable

FDA agrees with the Applicant’s description of compliance, concomitant and rescue medication.
Efficacy Results – Primary Endpoint (Including Sensitivity Analyses)
Data:
Study 20160323
At the 10 mg monotherapy target dose (N = 24 evaluable subjects in the Interim Efficacy
Analysis Set), 8 subjects had a confirmed response, including 2 subjects (8.3%) with a complete
response and 6 subjects (25.0%) with a partial response. The confirmed ORR (95% CI) based on
investigator assessment was 33.3% (15.6%, 55.3%). In addition, 1 subject had an unconfirmed
partial response awaiting confirmatory scan for a confirmed and unconfirmed ORR (95% CI) of
37.5% (18.8%, 59.4%). The response was rapid and durable, with a median (range) time to
response of 1.7 (1.6 to 11.3) months and a median (95% CI) DOR of 14.9 (3.0, NE) months. The
median (95% CI) follow-up time for DOR was 18.8 (1.0, NE) months.
171

Reference ID: 5382024

NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {BLA 761344}
{Tarlatamab}
At the 100 mg monotherapy target dose (N = 76 evaluable subjects in the Interim Efficacy
Analysis Set), 15 subjects had a confirmed response, including 2 subjects (2.6%) with a complete
response and 13 subjects (17.1%) with a partial response. The ORR (95% CI) based on
investigator assessment was 19.7% (11.5%, 30.5%). In addition, 2 subjects had unconfirmed
partial responses awaiting confirmatory scan for a confirmed and unconfirmed ORR (95% CI) of
22.4% (13.6%, 33.4%). The median (range) time to response was 1.8 (1.6 to 3.7) months; the
median (95% CI) DOR was not reached (5.6, NE) months. The median (95% CI) follow-up time
for DOR was 18.7 (12.8, 23.8) months.
Efficacy Update as of 02 October 2023
As of the 02 October 2023 data cutoff date, tarlatamab monotherapy and combination therapy
for SCLC subjects at multiple doses resulted in confirmed ORR (95% CI) of 22.2% (16.9%, 28.2%),
including 3 additional subjects with confirmed responses since the original cutoff date (49
subjects [02 October 2023] vs 46 subjects [28 March 2023]). One additional subject had an
unconfirmed partial response and was awaiting confirmatory scan. The ORR (95% CI) for the 49
subjects with confirmed response and 1 subject with unconfirmed partial response awaiting
confirmatory scan was 22.6% (17.3%, 28.7%). The DCR (95% CI) was 52.5% (45.7%, 59.2%)
(90-day Table 14-4.1.1 of Study 20160323). These updated results are consistent with the
results presented in the primary analysis (Section 2.2.3 of Module 2.7.3, Summary of Clinical
Efficacy).
The median DOR (95% CI) was 11.2 (6.6, 14.9) months for the 49 confirmed responders (90-day
Table 14-4.2.1 of Study 20160323).
Updated Kaplan-Meier plot for DOR is provided in 90-day Figure 14-4.3.1. An updated waterfall
plot of best overall response is provided in 90-day Figure 14-4.1 of Study 20160323. An updated
swimmer plot of time to response and duration of treatment among confirmed responders in
the Interim Analysis Efficacy Set is provided in 90-day Figure 14-4.2 of Study 20160323.
These results are consistent with the interim analyses presented in the initial marketing
application (Section 2.2.3 of Module 2.7.3, Summary of Clinical Efficacy), and this updated
efficacy information aligns with the overall study conclusions as originally presented (Study
20160323 Interim Analysis CSR).
The Applicant’s Position:
These efficacy results from phase 1 were supportive of those observed in the phase 2 study.
The FIH efficacy results across all dose cohorts are summarized in Section 2.2.3 of Module 2.7.3,
SCE; full results are provided in the Study 20160323 clinical study report.

Efficacy Results – Secondary and other relevant endpoints
Data:
Study 20160323
In the 10 mg target dose group, median (95% CI) PFS based on investigator review was 3.5 (1.9,
11.0) months, with a median follow-up time of 20.5 months. The Kaplan-Meier estimates for
PFS at 6 and 12 months were 36.3% and 24.2%, respectively. Median (95% CI) OS was 20.3
(5.1, NE) months, with a median follow-up time of 12.6 months. The Kaplan-Meier estimates for
OS at 6 and 12 months were 61.8% and 54.9%, respectively.
In the 100 mg target dose group, median (95% CI) PFS based on investigator review was 3.7
(1.9, 4.5) months, with a median follow-up time of 18.8 months. The Kaplan-Meier estimates
for PFS at 6 and 12 months were 32.1% and 18.6%, respectively. Median (95% CI) OS was not
reached (11.0, NE) months, with a median follow-up time of 12.0 months. The Kaplan-Meier
estimates for OS at 6 and 12 months were 79.3% and 60.4%, respectively.
Efficacy Update as of 02 October 2023
The Kaplan-Meier estimate of median (95% CI) PFS of all subjects was 3.3 (2.0, 3.7) months
(90-day Table 14-4.3.1 of Study 20160323). At the time of the data cutoff, the Kaplan-Meier
estimate of median (95% CI) OS was 12.8 (10.9, 20.3) months (90-day Table 14-4.4.1 of Study
20160323). These results are consistent with the interim analyses presented in the initial
marketing application (Section 2.2.3 of Module 2.7.3, Summary of Clinical Efficacy), and this
updated efficacy information aligns with the overall study conclusions as originally presented
(Study 20160323 Interim Analysis CSR).

8.1.5.

Integrated Review of Effectiveness

The FDA’s Assessment:
Efficacy data from Parts 1 and 2 of Study 20200491 in patients who received the tarlatamab 10
mg proposed dose comprise the primary dataset to support this application. See Section 8.1.2
for detailed discussion of these results. Efficacy data from Part 3 of Study 20200491 and in
patients who received the tarlatamab 10 mg dose in Study 20160323 were evaluated as
supportive evidence of effectiveness for this application.
The proposed indication by the Applicant (SCLC with disease progression on or after platinumbased chemotherapy) differs from the population studied in the 20200491 study (SCLC with
disease progression after receiving previous treatment with platinum-based chemotherapy and
at least one other line of prior therapy). To evaluate the appropriateness of the Applicant’s
proposal, FDA performed analyses of efficacy subgroups by prior therapies from Study
20200491 which are further described in Section 8.1.7.

Reference ID: 5382024

NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {BLA 761344}
{Tarlatamab}
8.1.6.

Assessment of Efficacy Across Trials

Primary Endpoints
Data:
See Efficacy Results – Primary Endpoint (Including Sensitivity Analyses).
The Applicant’s Position:
The primary evidence for efficacy for this marketing application is the evaluation of the data for
222 subjects with advanced SCLC who received tarlatamab monotherapy (10 or 100 mg) in the
pivotal phase 2 Study 20200491, as reflected in a durable objective response in a large
percentage of subjects and supported by the totality of clinically significant response measures.
The efficacy results from the 208 subjects enrolled in FIH Study 20160323 provided supportive
efficacy data.
The FDA’s Assessment:
FDA generally agrees with the Applicant’s position, with the exception that this review did not
rely on efficacy data from patients treated at the 100 mg dose. As described in Section 8.1.5,
the primary evidence for efficacy for this marketing application is from the evaluation of data
from 99 patients with ES-SCLC who received tarlatamab 10 mg from Parts 1 and 2 of Study
20200491. The efficacy results from Part 3 of Study 20200491, along with those from the 24
efficacy evaluable patients who received the tarlatamab 10 mg dose in Study 20160323 provide
supportive efficacy data.
Secondary and Other Endpoints
Data:
See Efficacy Results – Secondary and other relevant endpoints.
The Applicant’s Position:
Overall, efficacy data for the phase 1 study (20160323) are consistent with the phase 2
efficacy results (from Study 20200491) in subjects with advanced SCLC treated with
tarlatamab 10 mg monotherapy.
The FDA’s Assessment:
FDA generally agrees with the Applicant’s position. The ORR results from the 24 efficacy

Reference ID: 5382024

NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {BLA 761344}
{Tarlatamab}
evaluable patients from Study 20160323 are generally consistent with the Study 20200491
efficacy results.
Additionally, in Study 20200491, 34 patients received tarlatamab at the proposed dosing
regimen in Part 3 of the study, which was primarily intended to evaluate the safety of
tarlatamab under reduced monitoring criteria for CRS. The confirmed BICR ORR per RECIST v1.1
was 32% (95% CI: 17%, 51%). The DoR estimate is premature as 73% of the patients who had
responses were censored for the DoR analysis. These ORR results are generally consistent with
the results observed in Parts 1 and 2 (40% vs. 32%).
Subpopulations
Analyses for ORR, DOR, DoDC, PFS, and OS in Study 20200491 Parts 1 and 2 (10 and 100 mg)
were conducted to explore the consistency of tarlatamab treatment effect across subgroups of
age, race, region, number of prior lines of anticancer therapy, prior PD-1 or PD-L1 therapy, sum
of diameters of target lesions at baseline, platinum sensitivity, presence of brain or liver
metastasis at baseline, and DLL3 cut points. No notable differences were observed among these
subgroups for ORR (Figures 7 and 8 of Module 2.5, Clinical Overview), DOR, DoDc, PFS, or OS.
However, subgroup analysis interpretation may be limited because of the small sample size of
each subgroup.
A post hoc analysis performed for Parts 1 and 2 (10 and 100 mg) of the progression-free interval
after first-line platinum therapy, which included time to next therapy for subjects with
unknown progression dates after first-line platinum therapy, showed that efficacy was
observed across all subgroups. Objective response rate, DOR, DoDC, PFS, and OS across these
subgroups by progression-free interval after first-line platinum treatment were generally
consistent with the primary analyses (Table 4 of Module 2.5 [Clinical Overview] for ORR and
DOR).
A post hoc analysis performed for Parts 1 and 2 (10 and 100 mg) of prior lines of therapy
showed that efficacy was observed across subgroups of subjects who had (or had not) received
a variety of prior lines of therapy representing global standard of care, including topotecan,
lurbinectedin, and amrubicin. Objective response rate, DOR, PFS, and OS across these
subgroups by prior line of therapy were generally consistent with the primary analyses (Table 5
of Module 2.5 [Clinical Overview] for ORR and DOR).
Analyses were conducted for best overall response to evaluate concordance between
assessments by BICR (primary endpoint) and the study center investigator (secondary
endpoint). Of the 186 subjects, 164 subjects had scans evaluable by BICR and the investigator;
of these 164 subjects, 146 subjects (89.0%) were concordant (Table 14-4.2 of Study 20200491).
A post hoc subgroup analysis by sex and ECOG performance status at baseline also showed that
efficacy was observed across these subgroups; ORR, DOR, DoDC, PFS, and OS across these
subgroups were generally consistent with those from the primary analyses.
The FDA’s Assessment:

Reference ID: 5382024

NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {BLA 761344}
{Tarlatamab}
FDA generally agrees with the Applicant’s position. Forest plots of the subgroup analyses for
Part 1 and 2 (10 mg only) are presented in Figure 22. Review showed generally consistent ORRs
across multiple subgroups including age, sex, race, number of prior lines of anticancer therapy,
prior PD-(L)1 therapy, and DLL3 cutpoints. DLL3 cutpoints were previously validated by the
Applicant at the 25% and 75% cutoffs at moderate (2+) and strong (3+) staining intensity.
Review of efficacy data at these cutoffs did not show any differences in ORR across subgroups.
There were also 2 patients who were also DLL3 negative, with 1 patient responding to
tarlatamab treatment.

Reference ID: 5382024

NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {BLA 761344}
{Tarlatamab}
Although based on its mechanism of action a differential effect is not expected for tarlatamab in
patients with platinum-sensitive vs platinum-resistant disease, data was also reviewed
regarding patient’s sensitivity to platinum. Of the 99 patients who received tarlatamab 10 mg,
data on platinum sensitivity was available in 69 patients. The ORR was 52% (95% CI: 32%, 71%)
in 27 patients with platinum-resistant SCLC (defined as < 90 days after end of first line platinum
therapy to date of progression) and 31% (95% CI: 18%, 47%) in 42 patients with platinumsensitive SCLC (defined as ≥90 days after end of first line platinum therapy to date of
progression). The ORR for the 30 patients with data unavailable for platinum sensitivity was
43% (95% CI: 25%, 63%). Although the ORR point estimates differ between these subgroups, the
95% CI overlap, and given the known mechanism of action of tarlatamab as a targeted agent
that causes activation of the immune system, FDA considers that a true difference in efficacy
based on platinum sensitivity status is unlikely with tarlatamab.
Supportive analysis was performed by patient’s sensitivity to platinum using an alternative
definition where the patient’s progression free interval was defined as time from first line
platinum treatment to disease progression or start of second line treatment. Using this
alternative definition, the ORR was 45% (95% CI: 29%, 62%) in 38 patients with platinumsensitive disease and 38% (95% CI: 25%, 52%) in 55 patients with platinum-resistant disease
with a 50% ORR in 6 patients with unknown platinum sensitivity. This analysis is encouraging to
suggest that there is no difference to tarlatamab response by platinum sensitivity status.
Further information regarding this will be available from the confirmatory trial.
For this application the proposed indication from the Applicant (SCLC with disease progression
on or after platinum-based chemotherapy) differs from the population enrolled in Study
20200491 (SCLC with disease progression after receiving previous treatment with platinumbased chemotherapy and at least one other line of prior therapy). FDA conducted multiple
subgroup analyses based on prior therapies in Parts 1 and 2 of Study 20200491 in patients who
received the 10 mg dose. The ORR in patients who received 2 lines of prior therapy was 42%
(95% CI: 29%, 54%) in 65 patients, and ORR was 38% (95% CI: 21%, 56%) in 32 patients who
received 3 or more lines of prior therapy. Of these patients, 50 received a topoisomerase I
inhibitor (topotecan, irinotecan or belotecan) as a prior treatment while 47 did not. The ORR in
patients who received a prior topoisomerase I inhibitor was 46% (95 CI: 32%, 61%), while the
ORR in patients who did not receive a prior topoisomerase I inhibitor was 41% (95% CI: 27%,
56%). Given that topoisomerase I inhibitors (including topotecan) are considered standard
treatments for ES-SCLC with progression after platinum-based chemotherapy and topotecan is
the only therapy with traditional approval from FDA in this setting, FDA analyzed the prior
treatments received and the response rates in patients who did not receive a topoisomerase-I
inhibitor per the table below.
Table 36 Tarlatamab efficacy for second line therapies for patients who did not receive a prior
topoisomerase-I inhibitor

Reference ID: 5382024

NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {BLA 761344}
{Tarlatamab}
Updates made to the primary analysis dataset are described in the document submitted on
12 December 2023 (SN 0014) and in the accompanying tables, figures, and listings (Module
5.3.5.2). The updated data do not impact the primary endpoint of ORR by BICR as presented
in the BLA. A listing of updated tumor responses assessed by Investigator was provided in
Appendix 4 of the Response Document (12 December 2023, SN 0014).
The FDA’s Assessment:
FDA generally agrees with the Applicant’s position. An updated datacut for DoR was provided
as well to provide the median with data and is described in the FDA’s assessment of efficacy
data in Section 8.1.2.
8.1.7.

Integrated Assessment of Effectiveness

Data:

Data are presented above in Section 8.1.1. Efficacy data were not pooled across the phase 1
and 2 studies due to differences in study populations, number of prior cancer treatments,
tarlatamab dose, or other aspects of treatment (monotherapy vs combination therapy).
The Applicant’s Position:
Results from both Study 20200491 (phase 2) and Study 20160323 (phase 1) demonstrated the
clinical activity of tarlatamab in subjects with advanced SCLC, a disease state with high unmet
medical need. Both studies showed rapid, durable responses, and consistent median PFS and
OS; improvements in PRO assessments were also seen with tarlatamab treatment in the phase
2 study.
Overall, tarlatamab demonstrated a clinically meaningful durable objective response and
potential survival benefits of PFS and OS. Median OS and median PFS are considered potential
survival benefits and will be further evaluated in the ongoing phase 3 randomized,
active-controlled Study 20210004.
The FDA’s Assessment:
FDA generally agrees with the Applicant’s position that tarlatamab demonstrated clinically
meaningful durable objective response. Time-to-event endpoints such as PFS and OS, and PRO
endpoints are difficult to interpret in the absence of an appropriate control arm.
FDA considers the observed 40% response rate (95% CI: 31%, 51%) from patients enrolled to
Parts 1 and 2 of Study 20200491 who received tarlatamab at the proposed dosing regimen a
clinically meaningful improvement over available therapy. The response rates were supported
by a median DoR of 9.7 months with 68% of patients with an observed DoR ≥6 months and 40%
of patients with an observed DoR ≥12 months.

Reference ID: 5382024

NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {BLA 761344}
{Tarlatamab}
The observed ORR was generally consistent across most patient subgroups, defined by relevant
demographic and disease-related characteristics, including across subgroups of patients with
platinum-sensitive and platinum-refractory disease and across subgroups with various DLL3
expression cut-offs. Of the 99 patients who received tarlatamab at the proposed dosing
regimen, 69 patients had available data regarding platinum sensitivity status. In this subgroup
of patients, the ORR was 52% (95% CI: 32%, 71%) in 27 patients with platinum-resistant SCLC
(defined as < 90 days after end of first line platinum therapy to date of progression) and 31%
(95% CI: 18%, 47%) in 42 patients with platinum-sensitive SCLC (defined as ≥90 days after end of
first line platinum therapy to date of progression). Although the ORR point estimates differ
between these subgroups, the 95% CI overlap, and FDA considers that a true difference in
efficacy based on platinum sensitivity status is unlikely with tarlatamab.
For this application, the proposed indication, SCLC with disease progression on or after
platinum-based chemotherapy, differs from the population enrolled in Study 20200491, SCLC
with disease progression after receiving previous treatment with platinum-based chemotherapy
and at least one other line of prior therapy. Overall, the ORR and DoR data from the primary
efficacy population in Study 20200491, which enrolled a more heavily treated patient
population (3rd line or greater), is greater than the ORR and DoR observed with topotecan, the
only available therapy with traditional approval from the FDA for patients with SCLC previously
treated with first-line chemotherapy (ORR of 24% in platinum sensitive disease and 2-7% in
platinum resistant disease). Review of prior therapies in the primary efficacy population from
Study 20200491 showed that 52% of patients received a topoisomerase I inhibitor as secondline therapy. Of the remaining patients who did not receive a prior topoisomerase I inhibitor,
the grand majority (89%) received other second line therapies considered treatment options
per U.S. standard of care. The ORR in the subgroups of patients who received prior
topoisomerase I inhibitor; and who did not receive prior topoisomerase I inhibitor (with or
without patients who received standard of care therapies), were generally consistent across
subgroups and consistent with the ORR observed in the overall study population. Furthermore,
it is not expected that treatment with additional therapies following platinum-based
chemotherapy would increase the likelihood of response to tarlatamab relative to treatment
with tarlatamab used as second-line therapy following platinum-based chemotherapy. In light
of the observed results and particularly considering the ORR observed with tarlatamab in
patients with platinum-resistant SCLC, which far exceeds ORR reported with standard of care
therapies, the available data for tarlatamab supports the proposed indication for treatment of
SCLC with disease progression on or after platinum-based chemotherapy.
Data from Part 3 of Study 20200491 and from patients who received the tarlatamab 10 mg
dose in Study 20160323 were considered supportive of the primary efficacy results from Study
20200491.
These results provide substantial evidence of effectiveness of tarlatamab for the treatment of
adult patients with extensive-stage small cell lung cancer (ES-SCLC) with disease progression on
or after platinum-based chemotherapy.

Reference ID: 5382024

NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {BLA 761344}
{Tarlatamab}
and Cycle 1 Day 8 doses.
The Applicant converted safety data from Study 20160323 from CTCAE version 4.0 to
CTCAE version 5.0 as well as upgraded CRS grading from Lee et al, 2014 criteria to
American Society for Transplantation and Cellular Therapy (ASTCT) 2019 grading scale
when all data was available. FDA agrees that the safety data provided allows for an
appropriate analysis of adverse events and the characterization of the safety profile of
tarlatamab.
8.2.1.

Safety Review Approach

Data:
Not applicable
The Applicant’s Position:

The primary analysis of safety for the proposed indication is based on the integrated safety
analysis of pooled data from subjects with SCLC who were treated with tarlatamab
monotherapy at a dose of 10 or 100 mg Q2W in pivotal phase 2 Study 20200491 and FIH
phase 1 Study 20160323; this population is the primary focus in this section. Supportive
integrated safety data across all tarlatamab monotherapy doses (Studies 20200491, 20160323,
and 20200040) and all tarlatamab combination therapy doses (with pembrolizumab in Part C of
Study 20160323, AMG 404 in Study 20200439, and atezolizumab or durvalumab ± carboplatin
and etoposide in Study 20200469) are provided in Module 2.7.4, Summary of Clinical Safety
(SCS) and in the Integated Summary of Safety (ISS).
Demographics, baseline disease characteristics, exposure, adverse events, and other safety
assessments were summarized using the safety analysis set, which included all subjects
enrolled who received ≥ 1 dose of tarlatamab in monotherapy cohorts and ≥ 1 dose of any
investigational product in combination cohorts.
Throughout the tarlatamab clinical development program, safety was continuously evaluated
by Amgen through routine signal detection activities such as review of all serious adverse
events across all the clinical studies.
Four adverse events of interest (EOIs) were analyzed for tarlatamab: cytokine release syndrome
(CRS), immune effector cell-associated neurotoxicity syndrome (ICANS) and associated
neurological events, neurological events (nervous system disorders [system organ
class]/psychiatric disorders [system organ class]), and neutropenia. These events were clinically
monitored through protocol designated reporting of clinical signs and symptoms, as well as
scheduled assessments of laboratory tests.
The FDA’s Assessment:
FDA generally agrees with the Applicant’s description of the primary and supportive safety sets
included in the BLA. FDA’s safety review approach for the proposed indication was based on the
updated 90-day safety datasets (DCO of October 2, 2023) of pooled data from Study 20200491

Reference ID: 5382024

NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {BLA 761344}
{Tarlatamab}
and Study 20160323 in 187 patients with extensive stage SCLC who were treated with
tarlatamab at a step dose of 1 mg on Cycle Day 1 followed by the proposed dose of 10 mg on
Cycle 1 Day 8, Cycle 1 Day 15 and every 2 weeks thereafter. FDA reviewed but did not
independently verify data submitted for patients treated with tarlatamab at a dose of 100 mg
every 2 weeks.

8.2.3.

Adequacy of Applicant’s Clinical Safety Assessments

Issues Regarding Data Integrity and Submission Quality
Data:
The study centers were monitored by contract research organizations for Study 20200491 and
by Amgen staff and partners in Study 20160323. Monitors were responsible for reviewing
adherence to the protocol; compliance with GCP; and the completeness, accuracy, and
consistency of the data. Direct access to subject medical and laboratory records was permitted
to verify entries on the study-specific eCRFs.
Investigator staff training was provided by Amgen Development during investigator meetings,
initiation, and routine monitoring visits. The sponsor organized investigator and clinical
research associate meetings before study start and during the study to provide information on
the investigational product, the study rationale and design, responsibilities under ICH GCP, and
training on the detailed study requirements.
Central laboratories were used in the studies. Where local laboratories were used, their
participation in internal and external quality control, quality assurance, and accreditation
schemes was evaluated by the study monitors.
The investigators were responsible for all data entered in the eCRFs and documented their
review and approval of the data by signing a form verifying the validity and completeness of the
data. The investigator was responsible for appropriate retention of essential study documents.
Case report form data were captured (via data entry by study center personnel in an Amgen
database system [Amgen RAVE Electronic Data Capture]). Data quality checks were applied
using manual and electronic verification methods. An audit trail to support data query
resolution and any modification to the data was maintained.
The Applicant’s Position:
No issues were identified regarding data integrity or submission quality that had an effect on
the safety review.
The FDA’s Assessment:
197
Version date: June 2022 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)

{Tarlatamab}
FDA agrees that the information in the application, including safety datasets and individual case
narratives, was adequate to support the safety analysis. There were findings noted from the
GCP site inspections as described in Section 4.1. The Applicant conducted a 100% SDR/SDV for
all clinical sites that participated in Study 20200491 which yielded updated data for efficacy and
safety that did not impact FDA’s interpretation of the trial results in a meaningful way. The
updated 90-day safety datasets with DCO of October 2, 2023, were used for FDA’s safety review
and labeling.

Categorization of Adverse Event
Data:
Not applicable, please see Applicant’s position.
The Applicant’s Position:
Standard methodologies were used to categorize adverse events. The analyses used Medical
Dictionary for Regulatory Activities (MedDRA) version 26.0 for initial ISS analysis and MedDRA
version 26.1 for 90-day update. The severity of each adverse event was graded using National
Cancer Institute – Common Terminology Criteria for Adverse Events (CTCAE) version 5.0 for
Studies 20200491 and 20200469. The severity of each adverse event for Studies 20160323,
(b) (4)
was converted from CTCAE version 4.0 to 5.0, if applicable. A
description of the methodology used for this conversion is provided in the Clinical Events
Classification Charter (ISS Module 5.3.5.3). Events of interest were evaluated based on
standardized MedDRA queries.
The collection of adverse events in the clinical studies was also appropriate. The protocols
defined adverse events and serious adverse events, as well as the reporting procedures.
Adverse events were collected after enrollment through the safety follow-up visit. All adverse
events considered to be related to investigational product and all serious adverse events
regardless of relationship were required to be followed until stabilization or reversibility.
Treatment-emergent adverse events were defined as any adverse event with an onset date
between the date of first dose and up to and including 42 days after the date of last dose of
investigational product or the end of study date, whichever was earlier.
The FDA’s Assessment:
FDA agrees with the Applicant’s description of AE categorization. The FDA review was
completed using MedRA preferred terms (PTs) and CTCAE grade as well as custom grouped
terms (GT) when performing independent analyses of AEs.

{Tarlatamab}
The applicant upgraded all adverse events (AEs) excluding cytokine release syndrome (CRS) and
laboratory data, from CTCAE version 4.0 to CTCAE version 5.0 if all the necessary data was
available. CRS AEs graded using Lee, 2014 criteria were also upgraded to ASTCT 2019 grading
scale (Lee, 2019) if all the data was available but was limited to the availability of temperature
and vasopressor use as data regarding hypoxia/oxygen intervention were not collected and/or
consistently reported.
Routine Clinical Tests
Data:
In Studies 20200491 and 20160323, all protocol-required laboratory assessments, including
routine hematology and serum chemistry panels, were to be conducted in accordance with the
laboratory manual and the schedule of assessments in the protcols. Laboratory values were
graded using the current version of NCI CTCAE and converted if necessary (see above). Vital
signs, physical measurements, and electrocardiograms (ECGs) were also assessed during study
visits as outlined in the schedule of assessments. Clinically significant abnormal vital signs or
findings were to be reported as adverse events.
The Applicant’s Position:
The clinical monitoring of subject safety was considered adequate for the expected toxicities
associated with tarlatamab treatment. The time points and analyses of clinical tests were
defined in the study protocols.
The FDA’s Assessment:
FDA agrees that the clinical monitoring of patient safety was adequate. For Studies 20200491
and 20160323, multiple safety assessments were conducted on the days of screening and
infusion which included physical examinations, neurological examinations, vital signs, height
and weight, performance status according to Karnofsky and laboratory evaluation (hematology,
chemistry panels, urinalysis, and pregnancy tests).
For Study 20200491 during Cycle 1 (Parts 1 and 2) vitals were performed every 15 minutes
during infusion and during the first 2 hours after end of infusion (EOI), followed by every 30
minutes from 2 to4 hours after EOI, every hour from 4 to8 hours after EOI, 12 hours post-EOI,
20 hours post-EOI, 24 hours post-EOI. After 24 hours post-EOI, vital signs should be assessed
per institutional standards. For Cycle 1 (Part 3) vitals were assessed every 30 minutes during
infusion, during first 4 hours post-EOI, every hour from 4-8 hours EOI, 12 hours post-EOI, 20
hours post-EOI and 24 hours post-EOI.
ECGs were collected pre and post infusion as part of standard monitoring for QT prolongation.
Triplicate ECGs were collected pre-infusion on Cycle 1 Day 1 (C1D1) and post-infusion on C2D1

and C2D15 for the first approximately 50 patients in Part 1. ECGs were collected pre and post
infusion for those patients who did not have triplicated ECGs performed.

8.2.4.

Safety Results

Deaths
Data:

Monotherapy Studies
Overall, fatal adverse events were reported for 19 subjects (4.2%) treated with tarlatamab
monotherapy; the most frequently reported (> 1 subject) fatal adverse events were aspiration,
cardio-respiratory arrest, neuroendocrine carcinoma of the prostate, pneumonia, and
respiratory failure (2 subjects [0.4%] each (Table 44). The 2 fatal events of respiratory failure
were considered treatment related (ISS Table 14-6.2.15). Both subjects had significant
confounders.
In the 10 mg dose group, fatal adverse events were reported for 5 subjects (3.1%) with no
preferred term reported for more than 1 subject. In the 100 mg group, fatal adverse events
were reported for 6 subjects (3.7%); all events occurred in 1 subject except cardio-respiratory
arrest (2 subjects [1.2%]).
Combination Therapy Studies
No fatal adverse events were reported for subjects treated with tarlatamab combination

therapy in Studies 20160323 Part C

Serious Adverse Events
Data:
The incidence of serious adverse events in the monotherapy studies that occurred in ≥ 1% of
subjects at the doses of 10 and 100 mg are shown in Table 47.
The incidence of serious adverse events was generally lower across the combination therapy
studies (25.0% to 64.3%) compared with monotherapy (59.3%); the most frequently reported
serious adverse event with combination therapy was CRS (ISS Tables 14-6.2.5 and 14-6.2.6).
In Study 20200491 alone, serious adverse events were reported for 134 subjects (60.9%)
overall (58 subjects [58.6%] in the 10 mg target dose group in Part 1 and Part 2, 61 subjects
[70.1%] in the 100 mg target dose group, and 15 subjects [44.1%] in the Part 3 modified safety
monitoring 10 mg target dose group) (Table 48).

Overall, as of 02 October 2023, serious adverse events were reported for 289 subjects (59.7%)
treated with tarlatamab monotherapy. Consistent with the initial ISS analysis, the most
frequently reported (≥ 3% of subjects) serious adverse events by preferred term included CRS
(26.9%), pneumonia (4.1%), and pyrexia (3.9%).
For the 10 mg target dose, serious adverse events were reported for 104 subjects (55.6%) as of
02 October 2023. Consistent with the initial ISS analysis, the most frequently reported (≥ 3% of
subjects) serious adverse events by preferred term included CRS (23.5%), pneumonia (3.7%),
pyrexia (3.7%); in contrast, hyponatremia was also reported in ≥ 3% of subjects (3.2%).
For the 100 mg target dose, serious adverse events were reported for 103 subjects (63.2%) as
of 02 October 2023. Consistent with the initial ISS analysis, the most frequently reported (≥ 3%
of subjects) serious adverse events by preferred term were CRS (34.4%), ICANS (6.1%), pyrexia
