Dose Interruption/Reduction Due to Adverse Effects
Data:
Adverse events leading to dose reduction or interruption of tarlatamab were reported for
157 subjects (34.4) treated with tarlatamab monotherapy, with the following preferred terms
reported for ≥ 2% of subjects: CRS (6.1%), COVID-19 (3.9%), neutropenia (2.8%), fatigue (2.6%),
and pyrexia (2.0%) (ISS Table 14-6.2.17).
The incidence of adverse events leading to dose reduction or interruption of tarlatamab was
lower in the monotherapy 10 mg group (49 subjects [30.6%]) than in the 100 mg group (71
subjects [43.6%]). The most frequently reported (³ 5% in either treatment group) adverse
events leading to dose reduction or interruption of tarlatamab occurred at a lower incidence in
the 10 mg group compared with the 100 mg group: CRS (3.8% vs 9.2%), COVID-19 (3.8% vs
6.1%), and neutropenia (0.6% vs 5.5%).

In the monotherapy studies, the subject incidence of adverse events, grade 3 and higher
adverse events, and adverse events leading to discontinuation of tarlatamab were comparable
between the 10 and 100 mg doses. The incidences of serious adverse events and adverse
events leading to dose interruption or reduction were slightly lower for subjects treated with
10 mg compared with subjects treated with 100 mg (Table 53).
Adverse events that have been determined by the Sponsor to be associated with tarlatamab,
based on the totality of the data, are considered to be adverse drug reactions. The identified
adverse drug reactions are anemia, neutropenia, constipation, nausea, pyrexia, asthenia,
fatigue, CRS, neutrophil count decreased, decreased appetite, hyponatremia, dysgeusia, ICANS,
neurotoxicity, tremor, confusional state, delirium, and dyspnea.
The FDA’s Assessment:
FDA’s analysis of treatment emergent adverse events in the pooled safety population is provided
in Table 13.
The most common (>20%) adverse reactions were cytokine release syndrome (55%), fatigue
(51%), dysgeusia (36%), pyrexia (36%), decreased appetite (34%), constipation (30%),
musculoskeletal pain (30 %), anemia (27%) and nausea (22%).
A total of 393 previously unreported adverse events were identified in 111 patients following
100% SDR/SDV of adverse events in Study 20200491. As a result of the revised 90-day update
safety analysis, twenty-eight additional treatment-emergent adverse events were identified in
20 patients treated with tarlatamab at the 10 mg dose. Out of 28 adverse events, 14 Grade 3
adverse events were reported for 10 patients (7.5%). This included lymphopenia (n=4), general
health deterioration (n=2), activated partial thromboplastin time prolonged, acute kidney
injury, anemia, back pain, cholestasis, decreased appetite, hyperglycemia, and hyponatremia
(n=1 in 1 patient each). Two serious adverse events of anemia (one Grade 3 and one Grade 2)
were reported for 1 patient treated with tarlatamab monotherapy at the recommended dose of
10 mg.
The overall incidence of any grade CRS increased from 101 patients (54%) to 103 patients (55%)
in the 10 mg dose group with no changes in ≥Grade 3 CRS events. The 2 new CRS events in the
10 mg dose group were both Grade 1 and nonserious events. There were no changes in ≥ Grade
2 CRS events, serious CRS events, or CRS events leading to discontinuation of tarlatamab.
There were 2 new cases of ICANS, both were Grade 1 events. Overall, there were no changes in
≥Grade 2 ICANS events, serious ICANS, or ICANS events leading to discontinuation of
tarlatamab. In addition, there were 6 new cases of neurologic toxicities, peripheral sensory
neuropathy, leukoencephalopathy, insomnia (n=2 each), muscular weakness,
leukoencephalopathy and dysgeusia (n=1 each), which were all low grade (grade 1 or 2).

{Tarlatamab}

Laboratory Findings
Data:
In general, clinical laboratory evaluations (eg, serum chemistry, hematology) demonstrated no
clinically significant effect of tarlatamab (Section 3 of Module 2.7.4).
The laboratory parameters with a ≥ 3 grade change from baseline reported for ≥ 5% of subjects
treated with tarlatamab monotherapy were decrease in lymphocytes (24.3%) and decrease in
sodium (12.7%). A lower incidence of grade ≥ 3 decreases from baseline in sodium was
observed for subjects with SCLC treated with tarlatamab monotherapy at 10 mg compared with
100 mg (8.8% vs 16.0%) (Section 3 of Module 2.7.4, SCS). Adverse events associated with
neutropenia are discussed in Section 8.2.5.3. Neutropenia.
The incidences of liver function test abnormalities were low across all studies. Increases in AST
and ALT were mainly grade 1 and grade 2 and occurred in the setting of CRS (see Section
8.2.5.1. Cytokine Release Syndrome). There were no cases of drug-induced liver injury.

Safety Update as of 02 October 2023
Overall, the subject incidence and preferred terms of adverse events related to laboratory
abnormalities and changes from baseline in laboratory parameters in subjects treated with
tarlatamab monotherapy were consistent with the initial ISS analysis. Laboratory parameters
with ≥ 3 grades change from baseline reported for ≥ 5% of subjects treated with monotherapy
were decrease in lymphocytes from grade 0 to 3 (19.0%) and decrease in sodium from grade 0
to 3 (11.8%), consistent with the initial ISS analysis. No new cases that met the Hy’s Law criteria
were reported since the initial ISS analysis.
The Applicant’s Position:
There were no notable trends in laboratory evaluations, and the results were consistent with
the known safety profile of tarlatamab (Section 3 of Module 2.7.4 Summary of Clinical Safety).
The FDA’s Assessment:
FDA’s analysis of selected laboratory abnormalities that worsened from baseline in the pooled
safety population are provided in Table 39 below.

Electrocardiograms (ECGs)
Data:
Electrocardiogram (ECG) results are for Study 20200491 and 20160323. Overall, elevated
postbaseline corrected QT intervals (Fridericia) (QTcFs) were reported infrequently in subjects
treated with tarlatamab monotherapy, with maximum postbaseline QTcF intervals > 480 to
500 msec or > 500 msec reported for 2 subjects (0.4%) each. No subject with SCLC treated with
tarlatamab monotherapy at 10 mg had postbaseline QTcF intervals > 480 to 500 msec or >
500 msec. No subjects with SCLC treated with tarlatamab monotherapy at 10 mg or 100 mg had
postbaseline QTcF intervals > 500 msec.
Few subjects treated with tarlatamab monotherapy had changes from baseline in QTcF interval
> 30 to 60 msec (7.4%) or > 60 msec (0.9%) with similar incidences between the 10 mg and
100 mg groups (Section 4.2 of Module 2.7.4, SCS).
The Applicant’s Position:
Overall, the incidence of changes in ECGs was low and no notable ECG abnormalities potentially
associated with tarlatamab were observed.
The FDA’s Assessment:
FDA agrees with the Applicant’s description of the incidence and severity of QT prolongation.
No clinically significant QT prolongation was observed in the clinical and non-clinical QT
assessment. The data presented do not suggest a mechanism for QT prolongation by
tarlatamab.

At the proposed dosing regimen of 10 mg Q2W, treatment-emergent antitarlatamab incidence
was 12% (3 of 25 subjects) for the FIH Study 20160323 and 3.2% (4 of 124 subjects) for the
pivotal phase 2 Study 20200491, for a pooled total of 7 of 149 subjects (4.7%) across these
studies (ISS Table 14-10.1.1). In the pivotal phase 2 Study 20200491, which employed the
neutralizing assay, none of the subjects receiving the 10 mg target dose developed neutralizing
antibodies. Potential impact of antitarlatamab antibodies on the PK, clinical efficacy, and clinical
safety of tarlatamab was assessed with the following results:
•

Based on the population PK model, individuals with positive ADA binding status at any time
during the study were estimated to have approximately 21% higher clearance, which
translated to an approximately 24% decrease in serum trough concentrations on average
(see Sections 6.3.1 and 19.4.1).

•

The efficacy results (including ORR, DCR, and DOR) were comparable for antitarlatamab
binding antibody positive subjects compared with those who were antitarlatamab antibody
negative (see Section 6.3.1).

•

There was no impact of antitarlatamab antibody development on safety based on
assessment of adverse events (see Section 3.3 of Module 2.7.4, SCS).

8.2.5.

Analysis of Submission-Specific Safety Issues

8.2.5.1. Cytokine Release Syndrome
Data:
Monotherapy Studies
Using the AMQ narrow search, CRS adverse events of any grade were reported for 270 subjects
(59.1%) across all doses (ISS Table 14-6.1.7), with 265 subjects having resolved events and
6 subjects with ongoing events as of the data cutoff date (ISS Table 14-6.7.5). Serious CRS
events were reported for 122 subjects (26.7%), and 3 subjects (0.7%) had a CRS event that led
to tarlatamab treatment discontinuation.
In subjects receiving tarlatamab 10 mg monotherapy in the 2 clinical studies, using the AMQ
narrow search, CRS occurred in 53.8% of subjects, including (worst) grade 1 in 31.0%, grade 2 in
21.4%, and grade 4 in 0.8% of subjects. No subject had a grade 3 or grade 5 event of CRS.
Serious events of CRS were reported in 23.1% of subjects. There were no fatal CRS events.
After the first dose of tarlatamab (cycle 1 day 1), 41.3% of subjects experienced any grade CRS,
with 28.8% of subjects experiencing any grade CRS after the second dose (ISS Table 14-6.2.404).
The majority of CRS events occurred after the first 2 doses, with 8.8% of subjects experiencing
CRS following the third dose or later. Following the day 1 infusion, 15.6% of subjects
experienced ≥ grade 2 CRS. Following the day 8 infusion, 4.4% of subjects experienced ≥ grade 2
CRS. Clinical signs and symptoms of CRS included pyrexia, hypotension, fatigue, tachycardia,
headache, chills, nausea, and vomiting. The majority of these events did not lead to tarlatamab
discontinuation.
Subjects with SCLC treated with tarlatamab monotherapy at 100 mg had higher rates of grade
≥ 3 CRS events (6.1%) compared with subjects treated with 10 mg (1.3%) (ISS Table 14-6.1.7).
Otherwise, the overall frequency and severity of CRS events (ie, grade 2 and above, serious CRS,
and CRS events leading to discontinuation of tarlatamab) were generally similar among subjects
treated with tarlatamab monotherapy at 10 and 100 mg. Hence, the median time to onset and
duration of CRS are provided below for the overall pooled monotherapy studies with results for

Study 20200491
To provide the clinically most meaningful safety analysis, the following CRS analysis focuses on
CRS events in Study 20200491 reported for subjects treated with tarlatamab 10 mg with a 1 mg
step dose in cycle 1 where the data collected were more detailed for CRS. When meaningful,
data are also provided for subjects in the 100 mg group initiated by step dosing in cycle 1, since
the step dose of 1 mg is common to both treatment groups and will be used with the dose of
10 mg.
In Study 20200491, the incidence of CRS adverse events for subjects treated with tarlatamab
monotherapy at 10 mg and 100 mg was 40.6% and 28.7% on cycle 1 day 1; 29.3% and 49.4% on
cycle 1 day 8; 7.5% and 6.9% on cycle 1 day 15. The incidence of CRS events was lower in cycle 2
and beyond, ranging from 0 to 1.5% in the 10 mg group and 0 to 4.6% in the 100 mg group
(Table 14-6.5.401 of Study 20200491).
On cycle 1 day 1, after the 1 mg infusion, 31 of 220 subjects (14.1%) had a grade ≥ 2 CRS event
that generally required interventions of low flow oxygen and IV hydration (Table 14-6.13.402 of
Study 20200491). On cycle 1 day 1, grade ≥ 2 CRS events that required interventions including
low and high flow oxygen or IV hydration occurred before 24 hours in 16 of 220 subjects (7.3%),
and after 24 hours in 4 of 220 subjects (1.8%). The median time to first onset of grade ≥ 2 CRS
(AMQ, narrow search) was 25.9 hours from the first dose of tarlatamab (Table 14-6.10.2 of
Study 20200491).
On cycle 1 day 8, in the 10 mg group, there were 4 of 133 subjects (3%) that had grade ≥ 2 CRS
events that generally required interventions of low flow oxygen and IV hydration. No grade ≥ 3
CRS events were reported (Table 14-6.5.401 of Study 20200491). High flow oxygen (≥ 6 L/min)
was not used in events after the cycle 1 day 8 dose (Figure 14-6.1.401 of Study 20200491). No
grade ≥ 2 CRS events that required interventions including low flow oxygen and IV hydration

The Applicant’s Position:
Most cases of CRS were grade 1 and grade 2 events that could be conservatively managed.
Grade 3 and grade 4 CRS adverse events were rare. Most cases did not require measures such
as tocilizumab, oxygen, or vasopressors. In general, events of CRS did not recur after
subsequent doses of tarlatamab. In cases where it did progress, it took many hours to evolve
from grade 1 to grade 2 to grade 3, where intervention would be required; the median time to
first onset of CRS (AMQ, narrow search) from the first dose of tarlatamab was 26.0 and
55.3 hours for grade ≥ 2 and grade ≥ 3 CRS, and from the last prior dose of tarlatamab was
15.1 and 30.6 hours, respectively. There were few grade 2 and higher events or hospital-based
interventions used from cycle 1 day 15 and beyond.
The FDA’s Assessment:
FDA agrees that most instances of CRS were Grade 1 or 2 and that most patients experienced
CRS following tarlatamab administration of initial step up dose of 1 mg IV on Cycle 1 Day 1.
Patients received premedication with dexamethasone 8 mg IV administered 1 hour prior to
infusion on Days 1 and 8 of Cycle 1 only and post IV hydration with 1 liter of normal saline over
4-5 hours.
In the pooled safety population (n = 187), CRS occurred in 55% of patients, including 34% Grade
1, 19% Grade 2, 1.1% Grade 3 and 0.5% Grade 4 of tarlatamab-treated patients. Tocilizumab
was used in 9% of patients and there were no fatal Grade 5 events. Recurrent CRS occurred in
24% of tarlatamab-treated patients (18% Grade 1, 6% Grade 2).
Most events (43%) of CRS occurred after the first dose with 29% of patients experiencing any
grade CRS after the second dose and 9% of patients experiencing CRS following the third dose
or later. Following the Day 1, Day 8, Day 15 infusions, 16%, 4.3%, and 2.1% of patients

to 420). Decreased hemoglobin occurred in 58% including 5% Grade 3 or 4. Febrile neutropenia
occurred in 0.5% of patients treated with IMDELLTRA.

8.2.7.

Safety Analyses by Demographic Subgroups

Summaries of adverse events and treatment-related adverse events; subject incidence of
adverse events and treatment-related adverse events by preferred term; and subject incidence
of adverse events and treatment-related adverse events by system organ class were analyzed
by intrinsic and extrinsic factor subgroups of race (American Indian or Alaska Native, Asian,
Black or African American, Native Hawaiian or Other Pacific Islander, White, Other), ethnicity
(Hispanic/Latino, Not Hispanic/Latino – only for subjects reporting ‘White’ race), age (< 65 years
versus ≥ 65 years and < 75 years versus ≥ 75 years), sex, ECOG status (ECOG status 0, 1, 2), and
region (Asia, Europe, North America, rest of world).
Overall summaries of adverse events by subgroup are provided in Section 5.1 and Section 5.2 of
Module 2.7.4, SCS. In the integrated monotherapy analysis set, no notable safety trends were
observed across the subgroups examined. No meaningful differences were observed in the
types of adverse events or treatment-related adverse events reported in the subgroups
examined; however, small sample sizes in some subgroups limit the ability to draw meaningful
conclusions.
The Applicant’s Position:
Overall, subgroup analyses demonstrated a pattern of events consistent with that reported for
the overall study population.

Human Reproduction and Pregnancy
Data:
No clinical studies of tarlatamab have been conducted in pregnant or breastfeeding women. No
breastfeeding cases were reported with exposure to tarlatamab.
There are no clinical studies to evaluate the effect of tarlatamab on fertility.
The Applicant’s Position:
There are no available data from the use of tarlatamab in pregnant women. Based on its
mechanism of action, tarlatamab may cause fetal harm when administered to a pregnant
woman.
It is unknown whether tarlatamab is secreted in human milk. Because many medicinal products,
including antibodies, can be secreted in human milk, a risk to the newborns/infants cannot be
excluded. A decision must be made whether to discontinue breast-feeding or to discontinue
tarlatamab treatment taking into account the benefit of breast-feeding for the child and the
benefit of tarlatamab treatment for the woman.
There are no clinical studies to evaluate the effect of tarlatamab on fertility.
The FDA’s Assessment:

Data:

8.2.11. Integrated Assessment of Safety

The key risks with tarlatamab treatment are as follows:
Cytokine release syndrome
• Cytokine release syndrome events were reported in approximately half of the subjects who
received the 10 mg dose of tarlatamab across monotherapy studies. Most of the CRS events
were nonserious and mild or moderate. No fatal CRS events have been reported. In general,
CRS is manageable and reversible (interventions may include oxygen supplementation, IV
fluids, vasopressors, and/or anti-interleukin [IL]-6 therapy). In some instances, tarlatamab
treatment interruption or discontinuation may be required for reversibility.
Immune effector cell-associated neurotoxicity syndrome (ICANS)
• Across monotherapy studies, ICANS and associated neurotoxicity events have been
reported in < 10% of subjects who received the 10 mg dose of tarlatamab. Most of the
events were mild or moderate. No fatal events have been reported. Most of the events
were self-limiting without the need for treatment discontinuation or dose reduction.
These risks can be successfully managed by monitoring patients for signs and symptoms of CRS
and ICANS following tarlatamab infusion, with temporary interruption or discontinuation
depending on the severity of the event.
Tarlatamab was generally well tolerated, with a low rate of adverse events leading to treatment
discontinuation in pivotal Study 20200491 and FIH Study 20160323 (6.9% in the 10 mg dose
group).
Routine risk minimization activities (ie, risk communications through prescribing information or
product packaging) are considered adequate to address the safety concerns associated with the
use of tarlatamab.
The Applicant’s Position:
Tarlatamab is generally well tolerated, and risks associated with tarlatamab use can be
adequately managed through communication in product labeling, routine pharmacovigilance
activities, and routine risk minimization activities. Tarlatamab demonstrated an acceptable
safety profile in adult subjects with previously treated advanced SCLC.
The FDA’s Assessment:
FDA agrees that the safety data presented in the BLA are derived from the pooled safety
population from Study 20200491 and Study 20160323 in 187 patients with extensive stage SCLC

who were treated with tarlatamab at a step dose of 1 mg on Cycle Day 1 followed by the
proposed dose of 10 mg on Cycle 1 Day 8, Cycle 1 Day 15, and every 2 weeks thereafter. FDA
agrees that the CRS and neurotoxicity including ICANS can be managed through communication
in labeling (including a black box warning). In addition, enhanced pharmacovigilance and a

risks. Other significant adverse events included infections, hepatotoxicity and hypersensitivity

profile of tarlatamab is considered acceptable in the context of a life-threatening disease.
Analysis of safety was based on the 90-day updated safety datasets using DCO of October 2,
2023. Missing adverse events from Study 20200491 were assessed and did not change the
overall safety profile of tarlatamab. Refer to Section 8.2.4 (Treatment-emergent Adverse Events
and Adverse Reactions) for further information on unreported adverse events.
FDA carefully considered the need for Risk Evaluation and Mitigation Strategies (REMS) given
the major identified safety findings of CRS and neurologic toxicity including ICANS and have
determined that a REMS is not needed; please refer to Section 12 for details. Based on our
review, we have determined that the toxicity profile of tarlatamab can be adequately conveyed
through labeling. In addition, enhanced pharmacovigilance and a Dear Healthcare Provider
letter are non-REMS adjuncts recommended to help convey these risks.

SUMMARY AND CONCLUSIONS
8.3. Statistical Issues

The FDA’s Assessment:
There were no major statistical issues with this application. In the primary efficacy population
(n=99), the confirmed ORR per BICR was 40% (95% CI: 31%, 51%), which was considered
clinically meaningful, and the tumor responses generally appeared durable with a median DOR
of 9.7 (95% CI: 6.9, NE) months and a median follow-up time of 15.1 months. The analyses of
time-to-event endpoints such as PFS and OS are difficult to interpret in the absence of an
appropriate comparator.

8.4. Conclusions and Recommendations

The FDA’s Assessment:
The review team recommends accelerated approval of tarlatamab under the provisions of 21
CFR 314.510 Subpart H for the treatment of adult patients with ES-SCLC who have progressed
on or after platinum-containing chemotherapy.
The recommendation is based on the favorable benefit-risk assessment for tarlatamab
248
Version date: June 2022 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)
Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the
Applicant and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.
Reference ID: 5382024

NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {BLA 761344}
{Tarlatamab}
observed from Parts 1 and 2 of Study 20200491 (DeLLphi-301) and from Part 3 of Study
20200491 and Study 20160323 in ES-SCLC, a serious and life-threatening condition.
The primary efficacy population consisted of 99 patients enrolled in from Parts 1 and 2 of Study
20200491 who received tarlatamab at the proposed dose. Of the 99 patients, 73 patients (74%)
had received prior anti-PD-(L)1 therapy; of these 73 patients, 58 (59%) received anti-PD-(L)1
therapy in combination with platinum-based chemotherapy in the first line setting. The
confirmed ORR by BICR was 40% (95% CI: 31%, 51%), with a median DoR of 9.7 months (range
2.7 to 20.7+ months). Of the patients with responses, 68% of patients had an observed DoR ≥6
months and 40% of patients had an observed DoR ≥12 months. The observed ORR and DoR
were clinically meaningful, and responses were observed across subgroups of patients with
platinum-sensitive and platinum-refractory disease and across subgroups with various tumor
DLL3 expression cut-offs. Although the ORR point estimates differ between platinum sensitivity
subgroups (31% in 42 patients with platinum sensitive SCLC and 52% in 27 patients with
platinum resistant SCLC), the 95% confidence intervals overlap, and FDA considers that a true
difference in efficacy with tarlatamab based on platinum sensitivity status is unlikely.
In context of approved therapies in the U.S. for the proposed indication, the ORR and DoR data
from the primary efficacy population in Study 20200491, which enrolled a more heavily treated
patient population (3rd line or greater) is greater than the ORR and DoR observed with
topotecan, the only available therapy with traditional approval from the FDA for patients with
SCLC previously treated with first-line chemotherapy (ORR of 24% in platinum sensitive disease
and 2-7% in platinum resistant disease). Review of prior therapies in the primary efficacy
population from Study 20200491 showed that 52% of patients received a topoisomerase I
inhibitor as second line therapy. Of the remaining patients, the grand majority (89%) received
second line therapies considered treatment options per U.S. standard of care. The ORR in the
subgroups of patients who received prior topoisomerase I inhibitor, and who did not receive
prior topoisomerase I inhibitor (with or without patients who received standard of care
therapies), were generally consistent across subgroups and consistent with the ORR observed in
the overall study population. Furthermore, it is not expected that treatment with additional
therapies following platinum-based chemotherapy would increase the likelihood of response to
tarlatamab relative to treatment with tarlatamab used as second-line therapy following
platinum-based chemotherapy. In light of the observed results and particularly considering the
ORR observed with tarlatamab in patients with platinum-resistant SCLC, which far exceeds ORR
reported with standard of care therapies, the available data for tarlatamab supports the
proposed indication for treatment of SCLC with disease progression on or after platinum-based
chemotherapy.
Continued approval for this indication may be contingent upon verification and description of
clinical benefit in a confirmatory trial, conducted as a PMR. The Applicant proposes to verify the
clinical benefit of tarlatamab in the treatment of patients with SCLC who have disease

13 Postmarketing Requirements and Commitment
The FDA’s Assessment:
The following Post‐Marketing Requirements and Commitments were issued and agreed upon
with the Applicant.
Postmarketing Requirements:
1. Conduct a multicenter randomized clinical trial intended to verify and describe the
clinical benefit of tarlatamab in patients with extensive stage small cell lung cancer (ESSCLC) who have had disease progression on or after platinum-based chemotherapy.
Final Protocol Submission: 12/2022 (Completed)
Trial Completion: April 2026
Final Report Submission: August 2026
PMR Rationale
Currently, ORR supported by DoR from the 20200491 single-arm trial is the primary
basis for accelerated approval. The current available treatment (topotecan) was
approved over 15 years ago in a platinum-sensitive SCLC population only. Time-to-event
endpoints such as overall survival (OS) and progression-free survival (PFS) cannot be
adequately interpreted in single-arm clinical trials due to confounding effects of
heterogeneity of the patient population. Long term efficacy outcomes including OS from
a randomized controlled clinical trial are needed to confirm clinical benefit of
tarlatamab.
2. Conduct an integrated safety analysis of data from patients with extensive stage small
cell lung cancer to further characterize the long-term incidence, severity, and outcome
of the known serious risks of cytokine release syndrome, immune effector cellassociated neurotoxicity syndrome and neurologic toxicity. Include a comprehensive
analysis from all available data sources including but not limited to patient-level and
pooled analyses of ongoing and completed clinical trials. These data could also be
obtained from the confirmatory study titled “A Randomized, Open-label, Phase 3 Study
of Tarlatamab Compared With Standard of Care in Subjects With Relapsed Small Cell
Lung Cancer After Platinum-based First-line Chemotherapy (DeLLphi-304)”.
Draft Analysis Plan: January 2024
Final Analysis Plan: April 2025
Final Report Submission: August 2025
PMR Rationale
262
Version date: June 2022 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)
Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the
Applicant and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.
Reference ID: 5382024

NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {BLA 761344}
{Tarlatamab}
Tarlatamab is a half-life extended (HLE) bispecific T cell engager (BiTE®) that targets
both delta-like ligand 3 (DLL3) on target cells and CD3 on T cells. Adverse events of
special interest for tarlatamab include cytokine release syndrome, immune effector cellassociated neurotoxicity syndrome and neurologic toxicity.
The safety is primarily supported by pooled data from patients in Studies 20200491 and
20160323 with extensive stage small cell lung cancer (SCLC) who received tarlatamab
monotherapy at 10 mg. Patients received step up dose of 1 mg on Cycle 1 Day 1,
followed by target dose of 10 mg on Cycle 1 Day 8 and Cycle 1 Day 15 and every 2 weeks
thereafter. Among 160 patients with extensive stage SCLC treated with the intended
marketing dose of tarlatamab 10 mg in Studies 20200491 and 20160323, the incidences
of Grade 3-4 adverse events were 64% and fatal adverse events were 2.4%. Serious
adverse reactions occurred in 54% and adverse reactions led to dose interruptions
occurred in 27% and discontinuation in 8%. The most common reasons for treatment
delay (≥3%) were fatigue and cytokine release syndrome. The safety data with
tarlatamab is reflective of a median duration of exposure 14.4 weeks (range: 0.1 to 93.4)
with 17% exposed for 6 months longer and 14% exposed for greater than 1 year. Further
data is needed to identify and characterize safety with longer use of tarlatamab given
the intended administration until progressive disease or unacceptable toxicity. The goal
of this PMR is to provide additional safety data to further characterize the adverse
reactions of interest with longer-term use of tarlatamab, including, but not limited to,
cytokine release syndrome, immune effector-cell associated neurotoxicity syndrome
and neurologic toxicity.
Postmarketing Commitment
3. Conduct an integrated analysis from ongoing, completed, or planned clinical trials and
other potential data sources as appropriate enrolling a sufficient representation of
United States (U.S.) racial and ethnic minority patients that is reflective of the U.S.
population of patients with SCLC, to further characterize the efficacy, safety and
pharmacokinetics of tarlatamab in these patients. In the analysis, include a sufficient
number of patients enrolled in the U.S., and a sufficient number of racial and ethnic
minorities reflective of the incidence of SCLC in each subpopulation to allow for
interpretation of the results. The analyses should support comparative efficacy and
safety outcome analyses between the aforementioned populations and White patients.
Draft Analysis Plan: January 2025
Final Analysis Plan: April 2025
Final Report Submission: April 2027

Overall, the Applicant’s PPK analyses for tarlatamab is considered acceptable for the intended
analysis objectives and can reasonably well describe the observed concentration-time profile.
The Applicant’s analyses were verified by the reviewer, with no significant discordance
identified. In addition, the developed model is deemed acceptable for deriving exposure
metrics for E-R analysis and the model simulation results were acceptable to support the
labeling statements regarding the general PK information and the effects of intrinsic and
extrinsic factors on tarlatamab PK.
The simulated tarlatamab exposures following the Applicant’s proposed retreatment plan after
dose delay support their proposed repriming strategy, which is expected to effectively mitigate
the incidence and severity of rebound CRS following restart of tarlatamab.
19.4.3.2. PPK Assessment Summary
The Applicant’s Position:
Based on cross-study population PK analyses:
•

For a 73 kg typical white individual, tarlatamab clearance, volume of distribution of central
compartment, volume of distribution of peripheral compartment, and volume of
distribution at steady state were estimated to be 0.65 L/day, 3.44 L, 5.06 L, and 8.5 L,
respectively. The inter-individual variability for tarlatamab clearance and volume of
distribution of central compartment were 43.7% and 38.7%, respectively.

•

Like other biologics, body weight was found to be correlated with tarlatamab clearance,
with increase in body weight associated with increase in clearance. However, at the
proposed clinical regimen of 1 mg on day 1 followed by 10 mg on day 8, day 15, and Q2W
thereafter, body weight had no clinically relevant impact on tarlatamab efficacy as assessed
by confirmed ORR or DCR (Figures 15 and 16 of Report 157687 [Exposure-response Analysis
Report] [Module 5.3.4.2]).

•

Positive ADA binding status was identified as a significant time-varying covariate on
tarlatamab clearance. Individuals with positive ADA binding status at any time during the
study were estimated to have higher clearance. Overall, the impact of ADA was not clinically
relevant as displayed by subgroup analyses for efficacy endpoints in ADA positive and ADA
negative subjects in Study 20200491 (Section 11.3.1 of Study 20200491 and Integrated
Summary of Immunogenicity [Module 5.3.5.3]).

•

Race was found to be correlated with volume of distribution of central compartment;
however, race had a negligible impact on exposures.

Tarlatamab PK were not significantly impacted by other factors such as age, sex, ethnicity,
serum albumin, number of prior lines of therapies, baseline disease burden or ECOG status, or
renal or hepatic function.

after occurrence of cytokine release

The FDA’s Assessment:
Overall, the Applicant’s PPK model is generally acceptable for the purposes of characterizing PK
and predicting exposure following intravenous infusion of tarlatamab in patients with advanced
SCLC with disease progression on or after platinum-based chemotherapy.
The Applicant’s parameter values for the final model were generally precisely estimated.
Residual standard error (RES) for all structural model parameters were low (<10.7%). RSE for
covariate effect parameters were also low (<24.8%), except for the effect of ADA on CL (RSE of
51.4%). All variance parameters were also estimated precisely with RSEs lower than 11.4%.
Shrinkage of the inter-individual random effects was low to moderate (<30%). The predictions
and residuals are symmetrically distributed without any significant trends. The VPC plots
showed acceptable model predictive performance across different studies with a slight
tendency to overpredict tarlatamab concentration at 5th percentile during the elimination
phase.
No clinically relevant differences in exposure according to patient characteristics were
identified, including age (32-82 years), bodyweight (35-149 kg), sex, race (White and Asian),
ethnicity, albumin, number of prior anti-cancer therapies, ECOG score, tumor burden, ADA

The FDA’s Assessment:
The E-R efficacy analyses supports the proposed dosage of tarlatamab (10 mg) in patients with
advanced SCLC with disease progression on or after platinum-based chemotherapy.
ORR (Primary endpoint)
Graphical analyses for ORR suggested an efficacy benefit for patients with tarlatamab Cavg,first
cycle above Q1 (Figure 39 and Figure 40). Exposure efficacy analyses indicated a positive
relationship between tarlatamab exposure and ORR, with higher ORR observed at higher
exposure levels. The ORR appeared to plateau at dose of 10 mg and above. The model
estimated ORR probabilities were 26.2, 31.4 and 33.6% at doses of 3, 10 and 100 mg Q2W,
respectively (Figure 42). Additionally, the estimated DCR probabilities were 52.0%, 60.8% and
64.5%, respectively (Figure 42). The corresponding tarlatamab median Cavg,first cycle values were
312
Version date: June 2022 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)
Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the
Applicant and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.
Reference ID: 5382024

NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {BLA 761344}
{Tarlatamab}
0.2, 0.773 and 6.77 ug/mL at doses of 3, 10 and 100 mg Q2W, respectively. No significant
covariate was identified for both ORR and DCR.
Additionally, the exposure metrics (Cavg, first cycle) used in this exposure efficacy analysis were
derived from the PPK analysis based on the patients’ actual tarlatamab dosing records over the
first cycle and patient specific covariates identified in the PPK analysis. Considering the first
response (PR or better) was assessed approximately 6 weeks after the initial treatment of
tarlatamab (Figure 13 in section 8.1.2), the Cavg during first cycle has integrated the treatment
modification information including any treatment discontinuation or dose interruption during
the first cycle. Therefore, Cavg, first cycle is an appropriate exposure metric for evaluating the
relationship between tarlatamab exposure and ORR. Based on the identified E-R relationship,
the entire exposure range following administration of 1/10 mg has likely already reached a level
of exposure that may lead to a plateau response (Figure 42). This is consistent with the
observed data which indicated that dose interruptions did not appear to impact the tarlatamab
responses in the 1/10 mg cohort (For the detailed information, please refer to Table 23 in
Section 6.3.2.2).
DCR, PFS, OS, DOR and BTSR (secondary endpoints)
Overall, E-R analyses indicated that, across the evaluated exposure range, the secondary
efficacy endpoints (DCR, PFS, OS and BTSR) were significantly associated with tarlatamab Cavg,first
cycle and appeared to reach a plateau with exposure at 10 mg Q2W. This is consistent with the
results obtained for the primary efficacy endpoint (ORR).
The univariate CoxPH analyses for time-to-events (PFS and OS) also indicated significant
associations between tarlatamab Cavg,first cycle and PFS and OS (Table 71). The E-R trend for DOR is
not statistically significant possibly due to the limited sample size (~28 patients in each
quartile). The multivariate COX regression analyses identified the sum of tumor diameter at
baseline as a covariate that was significantly associated with higher hazard for OS. No other
significant covariates were identified for PFS (Table 72). Of note, FDA does not consider these
endpoints interpretable in the absence of an appropriate control arm (see Section 8.1.2) and
these analyses are considered to be exploratory.
Please note that the efficacy of tarlatamab was evaluated at two dose levels, 10 and 100 mg, in
the dose expansion cohorts in study 20200491. There was no clinically significant E-R
relationship for efficacy over the exposure range between 10 and 100 mg.
19.4.4.3. ER (safety) Executive Summary
The FDA’s Assessment:
The E-R safety analysis generally supports the proposed dosage of tarlatamab (10 mg) in
patients with advanced SCLC with disease progression on or after platinum-based
chemotherapy.

The FDA’s Assessment:
The E-R safety analysis generally supports the proposed dosage of tarlatamab (10 mg) in
patients with advanced SCLC with disease progression on or after platinum-based
chemotherapy.
Applicant’s exposure safety analyses are considered acceptable. In response to the FDA’s IR, the
Applicant conducted several additional analyses to further explore safety-related aspects of the
treatment with tarlatamab in patients with advanced SCLC with disease progression on or after
platinum-based chemotherapy. These analyses showed that there was a positive E-R
relationship between tarlatamab exposure and neutropenia or neurologic toxicity including
ICANS with a higher risk of any grade neutropenia or neurologic toxicity including ICANS at
higher exposure.
The E-R analyses for CRS could be potentially confounded by the mitigation approach (step-up
dosing regimen) utilized to manage the CRS risk at doses of 3 mg and above. Consequently, the
analysis results may not accurately reflect the true relationship between tarlatamab exposure
and CRS risk.
No apparent trend between tarlatamab exposure metrics and the incidence rate of any grade
ICANS was identified.
Additionally, a comparison of overall neurologic toxicity including ICANS between 10 and 100
mg target doses indicated a consistent relationship between tarlatamab dose, exposure and
neutropenia or neurologic toxicity including ICANS.
CRS
Based on the data from Study 20200491, the overall CRS incidence rates were 30% for Grade 1,
20% for Grade 2 and 1% for Grade 3 following administration of 1/10 mg tarlatamab, while the
CRS incidence rates were slightly higher following administration of 1/100 mg tarlatamab with
Grade 1 at 35%, Grade 2 at 24% and Grade 3 at 5%. The analyses conducted by the Applicant
showed no clear trends in E-R relationships for Grade ≥ 3 CRS. However, these findings are not
considered reliable due to the limited number of patients (16/412) who experienced Grade ≥ 3
CRS in safety dataset.
The additional E-R analyses for Grade ≥ 1 and Grade ≥ 2 CRS suggested a slightly positive trend,
with a higher CRS rate at higher exposure levels. This relationship may indicate that 1 mg may

The Applicant’s exposure safety analyses indicated a shallow positive trend, with a higher
percentage of patients experiencing Grade ≥ 3 neutropenia as tarlatamab exposures during the
first treatment cycle increased.
Considering that the majority of neutropenia events occurred later beyond the first treatment
cycle, the E-R relationships for Grade ≥ 1, Grade ≥ 2 and Grade ≥ 3 neutropenia were assessed
using the different exposure metrics, Cavg, Cmax and Ctrough, which were derived according to the
following conditions.
--Cmax and Ctrough were derived from the last dosing interval before the event time of
neutropenia AEs.
--Cavg was calculated from the last complete dosing interval over 2 weeks before the event
time of neutropenia AEs.
As shown in Figure 48, the incidence rate for Grade ≥ 1, Grade ≥ 2 and Grade ≥ 3 neutropenia
were found to be correlated with the exposure to tarlatamab, with a higher incidence rate of
neutropenia observed at higher levels of tarlatamab exposure. It appears that Ctrough was the
exposure metric most strongly associated with the likelihood of neutropenia events, as evident
from the lowest Akaike information criterion (AIC) value compared to Cavg and Cmax.
Multivariate logistic regression analyses indicated that ECOG status at baseline was associated
with higher probability of neutropenia. Figure 49 showed the model predicted mean probability
and observed Grade ≥ 1, Grade ≥ 2 and Grade ≥ 3 neutropenia events with increasing
tarlatamab exposure.

Neurologic toxicity including ICANS
In response to the FDA’s IR, the Applicant conducted the exposure safety analysis for neurologic
toxicity including ICANS (Grade≥1, Grade≥2 and Grade≥3) using tarlatamab exposure metrics,
Cavg, ss and Cmax, ss, which were derived according to the following conditions.
-- Cavg,ss: average concentration over the dosing interval based on nominal dosing on Cycle 2 Day
1
-- Cmax,ss: maximum concentration over the dosing interval based on nominal dosing on Cycle 2
326
Version date: June 2022 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)
Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the
Applicant and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.
Reference ID: 5382024

NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {BLA 761344}
{Tarlatamab}
Day 1.
As shown in Figure 51, a positive exposure response relationship was observed for patients
experienced Grade ≥ 1 and Grade ≥ 2 neurologic toxicity including ICANS. No trend was found
for patients experiencing Grade ≥ 3 neurologic toxicity including ICANS. Consistent with the
findings from graphical analysis, a statistically significant relationship was identified between
tarlatamab exposure and Grade ≥ 1 and Grade ≥ 2 neurologic toxicity including ICANS. Higher
tarlatamab exposure was associated with a greater incidence rate of Grade ≥ 1 and Grade ≥ 2
neurologic toxicity including ICANS. No statistically significant positive exposure response
relationship for Grade ≥ 3 neurologic toxicity including ICANS was identified, likely due to the
limited number of patients (41/412) who experienced Grade ≥ 3 neurologic toxicity including
ICANS in the safety dataset.
Multivariate logistic regression analyses indicated that the higher sum of tumor lesion diameter
at baseline and exposure to prior anti-PD1 treatment were associated with higher probability of
neurologic toxicity including ICANS.
In addition, it was observed that a higher proportion of patients experienced Grade ≥ 1(78%)
and grade ≥ 2(46%) neurologic toxicity including ICANS following a 100 mg target dose, relative
to 10 mg target dose (69% for Grade ≥1 and 31% for Grade ≥2 events). This trend was
consistent with the estimated E-R relationship when Cavg,ss was employed as a predictor
variable. The predicted mean probability of experiencing Grade ≥1 and Grade ≥2 neurologic
toxicity including ICANS at median Cavg,ss corresponding to either 10 mg or 100 mg dose levels is
comparable to the observed data (Table 75).

effect

ng/mL) and ULOQ (133 ng/mL) levels.
No matrix effect was observed as 80% of the spiked
samples recovered within the acceptance criteria.

the LLOQ (0.0203 ng/mL), LQC (0.0650 ng/mL) and

No matrix effect was observed.

stability

Freeze-Thaw
Tarlatamab in human serum is stable up to 3 freezestability
thaw cycles (-80°C +/- 10°C and RT).
ULOQ (133 ng/mL), MQC (2.00 ng/mL) and LLOQ
(0.0203 ng/mL) were tested.
Long-term storage Long-term stability of tarlatamab in human serum can
be guaranteed for up to 12 months at -80°C +/- 10°C
and at -20°C +/- 5°C for up to 9 months.
